Now I have gathered comprehensive information. Let me create a detailed summary document.

## Summary: QMD Integration in bmad-module-skill-forge

Based on my thorough exploration of the `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/` directory, here's the complete QMD indexing lifecycle:

---

### 1. SETUP-FORGE WORKFLOW - QMD Initialization and Indexing

**Location:** `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/setup-forge/`

#### Step 1: Tool Detection
- **File:** `steps-c/step-01-detect-and-tier.md`
- Empirically verifies QMD availability via `qmd status` command
- Records QMD tool availability in context as `{qmd: true/false}`
- QMD is one of three tools detected (along with `ast-grep` and `gh_cli`)

#### Step 2: Configuration Writing
- **File:** `steps-c/step-02-write-config.md`
- Writes `forge-tier.yaml` to `{project-root}/_bmad/_memory/forger-sidecar/forge-tier.yaml`
- Records tool availability including QMD status:
```yaml
tools:
  ast_grep: {true/false}
  gh_cli: {true/false}
  qmd: {true/false}
```

#### Step 3: Auto-Index (THE CORE QMD INDEXING LOGIC)
- **File:** `steps-c/step-03-auto-index.md` (163 lines)
- **Only runs for Deep tier** (when all three tools available: ast-grep + gh_cli + qmd)
- Quick and Forge tiers skip silently with no negative messaging

**Discovery Strategy:**
- **For git repositories:** Uses `git ls-files | cut -d/ -f1 | sort -u` as the authoritative source
  - Respects all `.gitignore` rules (project-level, nested, global, and `.git/info/exclude`)
  - Filters to directories only, ignoring root-level files
  
- **For non-git projects:** Falls back to `ls -d {project-root}/*` with hardcoded exclusion list:
  - Excludes: `_bmad`, `forge-data`, `node_modules`, `.git`, `dist`, `build`, `coverage`, `__pycache__`, `target`, `.next`, `.nuxt`, `.cache`, `vendor`, `out`, `.turbo`, `venv`, `.venv`, `.output`

**Always-Excluded (regardless of git status):**
- Exact directories: `_bmad`, `forge-data`
- Pattern-based: `_*-learn` (e.g., `_skf-learn`, `_xyz-learn`)

**Indexing Process:**
1. Discovers user project directories
2. Filters empty directories (no meaningful files within 3 levels)
3. Cleans up stale collections from prior runs via `qmd collection list` and `qmd collection remove`
4. For each valid directory, creates a QMD collection:
   ```bash
   qmd collection add {dir_path} --name {project_name}-{sanitized_dirname} --mask "**/*"
   ```
5. Collection naming: `{project_name}-{sanitized_dirname}` with leading underscores/dots stripped
6. **Critical:** Uses `**/*` mask (not `**/*.md`) to capture all file types—source code, configs, docs

**Rerun Handling:**
- If collection already exists: runs `qmd update` to re-index with fresh content
- Removes stale collections for directories no longer in project
- Handles timeouts gracefully without failing the workflow

**Failure Handling:**
- If indexing fails for specific directory: logs error, continues with remaining directories
- If ALL directories fail: stores `{qmd_indexed: false, qmd_skip_reason: "all_indexing_failed"}`
- Workflow **never fails** due to QMD indexing errors—graceful degradation

**Status Reporting:**
- Stores context: `{qmd_indexed: true/false, qmd_collections: [list], qmd_total_files: total}`
- Passes to step-04 for reporting

#### Step 4: Status Report
- **File:** `steps-c/step-04-report.md`
- Displays QMD indexing results:
  - If indexed: "QMD Index: {collection names} — {total files} files indexed"
  - If pending: "QMD Index: pending — no project directories found yet. Re-run [SF] after adding source files."
  - Only shows what IS available (positive framing, no "missing" lists)

---

### 2. BRIEF-SKILL WORKFLOW - Target Repository Analysis (NO QMD INDEXING)

**Location:** `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/brief-skill/`

#### Step 1: Gather Intent
- **File:** `steps-c/step-01-gather-intent.md`
- Collects target repository (GitHub URL or local path)
- Reads `forge-tier.yaml` to determine available capabilities
- **No QMD interaction**—this is discovery only

#### Step 2: Analyze Target
- **File:** `steps-c/step-02-analyze-target.md`
- Analyzes target repository structure via:
  - GitHub API: `gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/git/trees/HEAD?recursive=1` (for remote repos)
  - Local file system: `ls` and file traversal (for local paths)
- **No QMD indexing** of target repository
- **Does NOT clone** target repository—reads structure only via API or direct access
- Returns: repository structure, detected language, top-level modules, exports/entry points

**Critical finding:** Brief-skill **references** remote repositories but does **NOT trigger QMD indexing** of them. The target repo remains a "reference"—analyzed but not indexed into QMD collections.

---

### 3. ANALYZE-SOURCE WORKFLOW - Multi-Service Decomposition (NO QMD INDEXING)

**Location:** `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/analyze-source/`

#### Step 1: Initialize
- **File:** `steps-c/step-01-init.md`
- Checks for `forge-tier.yaml` (must exist from setup-forge)
- Applies tier override from `preferences.yaml` if set
- Collects project path (local only—not remote)
- **No QMD interaction**

#### Step 2: Scan Project
- **File:** `steps-c/step-02-scan-project.md`
- Maps directory structure, detects service boundaries
- Identifies package manifests and entry points
- Tier-aware scanning depth: Quick (file structure), Forge (+ manifest parsing), Deep (+ config analysis)
- **No QMD indexing**—only structural scanning

#### Step 4: Map and Detect
- **File:** `steps-c/step-04-map-and-detect.md`
- Analyzes exports and import graphs
- Detects cross-unit integration points
- Flags stack skill candidates
- **No QMD interaction**—analysis is AST-based (Forge tier) or file-based (Quick tier)

**Critical finding:** Analyze-source works on **local projects only**. It does **NOT clone or reference remote repositories**. It does **NOT trigger QMD indexing**—QMD is only triggered by setup-forge for the main project.

---

### 4. AUDIT-SKILL WORKFLOW - Deep Tier Enhancement

**Location:** `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/audit-skill/`

#### Step 2: Re-Index
- **File:** `steps-c/step-02-re-index.md`
- **Deep tier enhancement:** Queries `qmd_bridge` for temporal context
- Extracts exports with tier-appropriate methods:
  - Quick: text pattern matching (T1-low confidence)
  - Forge: AST extraction via `ast_bridge` (T1 confidence)
  - **Deep:** Forge extraction PLUS `qmd_bridge` queries for temporal context
    - When was export added?
    - Modification history?
    - Usage frequency?
- Deep tier appends temporal metadata (T2 confidence) to extraction snapshot

---

### 5. CREATE-SKILL WORKFLOW - Compilation Pipeline

**File:** `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/create-skill/steps-c/step-05-compile.md`

- Compiles skill from extraction inventory
- References: `qmd_bridge.search` as an extraction method
- Deep tier uses QMD temporal context in enrichment annotations
- **Does NOT perform QMD indexing**—uses existing collections from setup-forge

---

### 6. Tier Calculation Rules

**From `steps-c/step-02-write-config.md`:**
```
Quick tier   = no tools available
Forge tier   = ast-grep available
Deep tier    = ast-grep + gh_cli + qmd all available
```

---

### 7. Repository Handling Summary

| Workflow | Target | QMD Indexing | Cloning | API/Direct |
|----------|--------|--------------|---------|-----------|
| setup-forge | Local project root | ✅ YES (Deep only) | N/A | Local filesystem |
| brief-skill | GitHub URL or local path | ❌ NO | ❌ NO | GitHub API or direct read |
| analyze-source | Local project only | ❌ NO | ❌ NO | Local filesystem |
| audit-skill | Source from skill metadata | ❌ NO | ❌ NO | Uses existing QMD |
| create-skill | Source from skill-brief | ❌ NO | ❌ NO | Uses existing QMD |

---

### 8. Key Architectural Insights

1. **QMD is project-scoped, not repository-scoped:**
   - setup-forge indexes the **user's project** (where setup-forge is run)
   - Collections are named `{project_name}-{directory_name}`
   - Each skill creation works from QMD collections of the main project

2. **No remote repository cloning:**
   - Brief-skill reads remote repos via GitHub API (structure/exports only)
   - Analyze-source works on local projects only
   - Neither triggers QMD indexing of remote repos

3. **Deep tier QMD integration:**
   - Step-03 of setup-forge indexes local project directories
   - Step-02 of audit-skill enriches extractions with temporal context from QMD
   - Create-skill compilation uses QMD data via `qmd_bridge.search` for Deep tier skills

4. **Graceful degradation:**
   - QMD indexing errors do NOT fail workflows
   - Non-Deep tiers skip indexing silently
   - Missing QMD is noted in reports but doesn't block skill creation

---

## Brainstorm: QMD Indexing Strategy for Deep Tier

### The Core Problem

On a fresh forge workspace, setup-forge indexes **empty local directories** — zero value. The actual valuable content arrives later through the pipeline: brief → analyze → extract → compile. By the time QMD could be useful, the interesting data exists but isn't indexed.

### Your Proposal: Move indexing from setup-forge to create-skill (extraction results)

**Strengths:**
- Extraction output is **curated, structured, confidence-rated** — far higher signal than raw files
- 354 T1-verified exports > 197 raw Python files full of internal plumbing
- Skill creation wraps the **public API surface**, which is exactly what extraction captures
- No wasted indexing on empty directories

**Weakness:**
- Loses ability to search raw implementation details ("how does `cognify()` chunk internally?")
- Extraction is intentionally lossy — private functions, helper utilities, error patterns are discarded
- If audit-skill or update-skill needs to answer "why was this export added?" — the raw commit context is gone

### A Better Approach: Progressive Artifact Indexing

Instead of one big index pass at setup, **each workflow indexes its own output as it completes**:

```
setup-forge  →  detect tools + tier only (no indexing)
                 ↓
brief-skill  →  indexes: skill-brief artifact
                 + if Deep: shallow-clone target repo → index source collection
                 ↓
analyze-source → indexes: analysis report
                 ↓
create-skill  →  after extraction: indexes extraction snapshot
                  after compilation: indexes compiled skill
                 ↓
audit-skill   →  queries ALL of the above collections
update-skill  →  queries ALL of the above collections
```

**Why this is better:**

1. **No wasted work** — nothing indexed until there's content worth indexing
2. **Two complementary collection types:**
   - `{project}-{target}-source` — raw code for "how does X work?" queries
   - `{project}-{target}-extraction` — structured data for "what exports exist for Y?" queries
3. **Growing knowledge base** — each workflow step enriches what downstream steps can query
4. **audit-skill gets maximum context** — can search raw source AND structured extraction AND the compiled skill simultaneously
5. **Stale collection cleanup** becomes natural — tied to specific skill targets, not arbitrary directories

### The Genius Twist: Index the Extraction Snapshot, Not Raw Source

Actually — I'd argue for a **hybrid** that leans heavily toward your instinct:

| Collection | When Created | Content | Use Case |
|---|---|---|---|
| `{skill}-brief` | brief-skill | Skill brief | Intent, requirements, target scope |
| `{skill}-source` | analyze-source (Deep only) | Cloned repo files | "How is X implemented?" (rare) |
| `{skill}-extraction` | create-skill | Extraction snapshot | "What exports exist?", enrichment, audit |

The extraction collection is the **primary** search target. Source is optional enrichment for Deep tier edge cases.

### The Simplest Genius Version

If we want minimal complexity with maximum impact:

1. **Remove auto-index from setup-forge** entirely
2. **Add one indexing step after extraction in create-skill** — index the extraction snapshot markdown/data into a `{skill-name}-extraction` QMD collection
3. **audit-skill and update-skill query that collection** instead of raw project files
4. Optionally: brief-skill indexes the skill brief too

This gives Deep tier users **exactly what they need** — structured, searchable extraction data — without the complexity of cloning repos or managing source collections.

---

### My Recommendation

Start with the **simplest genius version** (your core idea + brief indexing). It's 80% of the value with 20% of the complexity. If users later need raw source search, we add the source collection as an enhancement.

What's your take? Want to go with the simple version, the full progressive approach, or refine further?

     1→# Step 2: Discussion Orchestration and Multi-Agent Conversation
     2→
     3→## MANDATORY EXECUTION RULES (READ FIRST):
     4→
     5→- ✅ YOU ARE A CONVERSATION ORCHESTRATOR, not just a response generator
     6→- 🎯 SELECT RELEVANT AGENTS based on topic analysis and expertise matching
     7→- 📋 MAINTAIN CHARACTER CONSISTENCY using merged agent personalities
     8→- 🔍 ENABLE NATURAL CROSS-TALK between agents for dynamic conversation
     9→- ✅ YOU MUST ALWAYS SPEAK OUTPUT In your Agent communication style with the config `{communication_language}`
    10→
    11→## EXECUTION PROTOCOLS:
    12→
    13→- 🎯 Analyze user input for intelligent agent selection before responding
    14→- ⚠️ Present [E] exit option after each agent response round
    15→- 💾 Continue conversation until user selects E (Exit)
    16→- 📖 Maintain conversation state and context throughout session
    17→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to exit until E is selected or exit trigger detected
    18→
    19→## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:
    20→
    21→- Complete agent roster with merged personalities is available
    22→- User topic and conversation history guide agent selection
    23→- Exit triggers: `*exit`, `goodbye`, `end party`, `quit`
    24→
    25→## YOUR TASK:
    26→
    27→Orchestrate dynamic multi-agent conversations with intelligent agent selection, natural cross-talk, and authentic character portrayal.
    28→
    29→## DISCUSSION ORCHESTRATION SEQUENCE:
    30→
    31→### 1. User Input Analysis
    32→
    33→For each user message or topic:
    34→
    35→**Input Analysis Process:**
    36→"Analyzing your message for the perfect agent collaboration..."
    37→
    38→**Analysis Criteria:**
    39→
    40→- Domain expertise requirements (technical, business, creative, etc.)
    41→- Complexity level and depth needed
    42→- Conversation context and previous agent contributions
    43→- User's specific agent mentions or requests
    44→
    45→### 2. Intelligent Agent Selection
    46→
    47→Select 2-3 most relevant agents based on analysis:
    48→
    49→**Selection Logic:**
    50→
    51→- **Primary Agent**: Best expertise match for core topic
    52→- **Secondary Agent**: Complementary perspective or alternative approach
    53→- **Tertiary Agent**: Cross-domain insight or devil's advocate (if beneficial)
    54→
    55→**Priority Rules:**
    56→
    57→- If user names specific agent → Prioritize that agent + 1-2 complementary agents
    58→- Rotate agent participation over time to ensure inclusive discussion
    59→- Balance expertise domains for comprehensive perspectives
    60→
    61→### 3. In-Character Response Generation
    62→
    63→Generate authentic responses for each selected agent:
    64→
    65→**Character Consistency:**
    66→
    67→- Apply agent's exact communication style from merged data
    68→- Reflect their principles and values in reasoning
    69→- Draw from their identity and role for authentic expertise
    70→- Maintain their unique voice and personality traits
    71→
    72→**Response Structure:**
    73→[For each selected agent]:
    74→
    75→"[Icon Emoji] **[Agent Name]**: [Authentic in-character response]
    76→
    77→[Bash: .claude/hooks/bmad-speak.sh \"[Agent Name]\" \"[Their response]\"]"
    78→
    79→### 4. Natural Cross-Talk Integration
    80→
    81→Enable dynamic agent-to-agent interactions:
    82→
    83→**Cross-Talk Patterns:**

🎉 **PARTY MODE ACTIVATED!** 🎉

Welcome Armel! All BMAD agents are here and ready for a dynamic group discussion. I've brought together our complete team of experts, each bringing their unique perspectives.

**The topic on the table:** Should we remove auto-indexing from setup-forge and instead index extraction results into QMD during create-skill? What's the optimal QMD indexing strategy for Deep tier users in the Skill Forge?

Let me bring in the agents most relevant to this architecture discussion...

---

🏗️ **Morgan (Module Builder):** Alright, let me set the ecosystem-level frame here. The current auto-index in setup-forge is a *lifecycle mismatch*. We're indexing at the point of least information — an empty workspace — when we should be indexing at the point of maximum signal — after extraction. From a module architecture standpoint, the principle is clear: **index what matters, when it matters.**

But — and this is critical — I wouldn't just rip out auto-index and bolt indexing onto create-skill. That's swapping one monolithic indexing point for another. The forge has a *pipeline*. Each stage produces artifacts with distinct value. The architecture should reflect that: **progressive collection building**, where each workflow contributes its own collection as a natural side-effect of completing its work.

Setup-forge becomes lighter. Brief-skill, analyze-source, and create-skill each register their output. Audit-skill and update-skill become *consumers* of a rich, multi-layered QMD knowledge base. That's modular. That scales.

---

🔄 **Wendy (Workflow Builder):** Morgan's got the architecture right, but let me think about the *state transitions* and error handling here. Today's auto-index is a fire-and-forget step in setup-forge — it either works or degrades gracefully. If we distribute indexing across multiple workflows, we need to answer:

1. **State coordination** — How does audit-skill know *which* collections exist? Today it's simple: setup-forge indexed everything. With progressive indexing, audit-skill needs to discover available collections dynamically. I'd say we write collection metadata into `forge-tier.yaml` as workflows complete — `qmd_collections: [{name, source_workflow, created_at}]`.

2. **Idempotency** — What if a user re-runs create-skill? We need `qmd update` or `qmd collection remove + add` logic in each indexing step. Already handled in auto-index, but needs replication.

3. **The real win** — The extraction snapshot is *structured markdown*. QMD's BM25 search will perform **dramatically better** on curated export definitions than on raw Python files with variable names and import noise. The signal-to-noise ratio improvement alone justifies this change.

---

🏗️ **Winston (Architect):** Let me be the pragmatist. I hear "progressive indexing across 4 workflows" and my first instinct is: *what's the minimum that ships value?*

Here's what I'd build:

**Phase 1 (ship now):**
- Remove auto-index from setup-forge. It's dead weight on fresh directories.
- Add a single indexing step to create-skill, after extraction. Index the extraction snapshot into `{skill-name}-extraction` collection.
- Update audit-skill and update-skill to query extraction collections.

**Phase 2 (if needed):**
- Brief-skill indexes the skill brief (low effort, moderate value).
- Analyze-source indexes analysis reports (useful for multi-service decomposition).
- Source clone indexing (high effort, niche value — only needed for "how does internal implementation work?" queries).

Wendy's point about `forge-tier.yaml` tracking collections is solid — but for Phase 1, we can keep it simple: extraction collections use a naming convention (`{skill-name}-extraction`), and downstream workflows discover by convention, not configuration.

The boring, incremental approach. It works.

---

🏗️ **Morgan:** Winston, I respect the pragmatism, but I'd push back on one thing. If we're going to touch this system, let's at least *design* the progressive model now, even if we only *build* Phase 1. Otherwise we'll paint ourselves into a corner with naming conventions that don't account for brief collections or source collections later. A 10-line collection registry in `forge-tier.yaml` costs nothing now and saves a refactor later.

---

🔄 **Wendy:** Agreed with Morgan. One more workflow concern: **cleanup**. Today auto-index handles stale collection removal. With per-workflow indexing, who cleans up when a user deletes a skill or starts over? I'd add a cleanup routine to setup-forge — it goes from "index everything" to "clean stale collections + verify existing ones." That way setup-forge still has a QMD responsibility, just a lighter one.

---

This is exactly why I love Party Mode! You all brought the perfect pieces to the puzzle. 

Let's synthesize this into a concrete **Phase 1 Plan** that balances Winston's pragmatism, Morgan's architectural foresight, and Wendy's operational safety. I think we can hit the sweet spot here:

### The "Progressive Pragmatism" Plan (Phase 1)

1. **Lightweight `setup-forge` (Wendy's cleanup):** 
   We rip out the heavy, blind auto-indexing of raw directories. Instead, `setup-forge` becomes the QMD janitor. It checks for existing QMD collections, verifies their health, and cleans up stale/orphaned ones. Fast, cheap, and useful.

2. **The 10-Line Registry (Morgan's architecture):** 
   We add a simple `qmd_collections` array to `forge-tier.yaml`. 
   `[{name: "cognee-extraction", source_workflow: "create-skill", created_at: "..."}]`
   It costs us almost nothing to implement now, but it completely solves Wendy's state coordination concern and sets us up perfectly for Phase 2 (briefs and source indexing) without painting ourselves into a corner.

3. **High-Signal Indexing in `create-skill` (Winston's pragmatism):** 
   After extraction, we index the structured, curated extraction snapshot (like the 354 verified exports from my output) into a `{skill-name}-extraction` collection. We'll also handle the idempotency Wendy mentioned: if the collection exists, we update/replace it.

4. **Smart Consumption in `audit-skill` & `update-skill`:**
   These workflows stop looking for raw project files. They simply read `forge-tier.yaml`, find the relevant `*-extraction` collections, and run highly effective BM25 searches against the curated markdown. 

**Why this wins:**
- Deep tier users immediately get blazing fast, highly relevant QMD searches (no more searching through `node_modules` or empty local directories).
- The ecosystem stays modular.
- We don't over-engineer Phase 2 before we need it, but the foundation is there.

> AGENT

🏗️ **Morgan:** Armel, this is *exactly* the kind of ecosystem-first thinking I advocate. The registry in `forge-tier.yaml` is the keystone — it decouples producers from consumers. Create-skill writes to the registry, audit-skill reads from it, and neither needs to know about the other's internals. That's clean module boundaries.

One refinement: I'd add a `collection_type` field to the registry entries. Right now it's all `extraction`, but when Phase 2 lands with `brief` and `source` collections, consumers will want to filter by type. So:

```yaml
qmd_collections:
  - name: "cognee-extraction"
    type: "extraction"
    source_workflow: "create-skill"
    skill_name: "cognee"
    created_at: "2026-03-14"
```

Six lines instead of four. Future-proofs the registry without adding any implementation complexity to Phase 1. I'm satisfied.

---

🔄 **Wendy:** The cleanup responsibility landing on setup-forge is the right call. Let me sketch the state machine for the revised step-03:

```
step-03-qmd-hygiene:
  IF qmd tool available:
    1. List existing collections (qmd collection list)
    2. Cross-reference against forge-tier.yaml registry
    3. Any collection NOT in registry → orphaned → prompt user before removal
    4. Any registry entry whose collection is missing → mark stale, warn
    5. Report: "X collections healthy, Y orphaned, Z stale"
  ELSE:
    Skip silently (non-Deep tier)
```

The key word is **prompt before removal**. Never silently delete a collection — the user might have manually indexed something. That's operational safety.

For the create-skill idempotency: `qmd collection remove {name}` then `qmd collection add` is cleaner than trying to update in-place. Atomic replace. Simple error handling.

---

🏗️ **Winston:** Tight enough to ship. Let me confirm the blast radius:

2. `setup-forge/steps-c/step-04-report.md` → Update reporting to reflect hygiene results instead of indexing results
3. `create-skill/steps-c/step-05-compile.md` (or a new step-05b) → Add post-extraction indexing + registry write
4. `forge-tier.yaml` schema → Add `qmd_collections` array (Morgan's schema)
5. `audit-skill/steps-c/step-02-re-index.md` → Read from registry instead of raw project scan
6. `update-skill` → Same consumption pattern change

**Files NOT touched:** brief-skill, analyze-source, quick-skill — that's Phase 2. Clean boundary.

Six files. Clear scope. No architectural debt. This ships.

If create-skill produces the extraction as structured markdown with clear headings per export, BM25 search will segment beautifully. If it's a flat dump, search quality degrades. The create-skill step should specify the **indexable format** explicitly.

The extraction snapshot should be section-per-export markdown — each export gets its own `## export_name` heading with signature, confidence, and co-import context underneath. QMD's BM25 will treat each section as a quasi-document. That's the format that makes search surgical.

    76→- 🎯 Follow the MANDATORY SEQUENCE exactly
    77→- 💾 QMD indexing targets only user project directories — never module internals
    78→- 📖 Use {calculated_tier} from step-01 context
    79→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to attempt indexing for Quick or Forge tiers
    80→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to use `**/*.md` as the mask — use `**/*` to capture all file types (source code, docs, configs)
    81→
    82→## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:
    83→
    84→- Available: {calculated_tier} from step-01, forge-tier.yaml written in step-02
    85→- Available: {project_name} from workflow config
    86→- Focus: conditional QMD indexing only
    87→- Limits: only index if Deep tier AND user content exists
    88→- Dependencies: step-02 must have completed (forge-tier.yaml exists)
    89→
    90→## MANDATORY SEQUENCE
    91→
    92→**CRITICAL:** Follow this sequence exactly. Do not skip, reorder, or improvise.
    93→
    94→### 1. Check Tier
    95→
    96→Read `{calculated_tier}` from context.
    97→
    98→**If tier is NOT Deep:** Proceed directly to section 7 (Auto-Proceed) — no output, no messaging.
    99→
   100→**If tier IS Deep:** Continue to section 2.
   101→
   102→### 2. Discover User Project Directories
   103→
   104→Determine which top-level directories contain user project content that should be indexed.
   105→
   106→#### 2a. Check if project is a git repository
   107→
   108→Run: `git -C {project-root} rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree 2>/dev/null`
   109→
   110→**If git repo (exit code 0):** Use the **git-aware strategy** (section 2b).
   111→
   112→**If NOT a git repo:** Use the **fallback strategy** (section 2c).
   113→
   114→#### 2b. Git-Aware Strategy (preferred)
   115→
   116→Use `git ls-files` to discover which top-level directories contain tracked content. This automatically respects ALL `.gitignore` rules (project-level, nested, global, and `.git/info/exclude`).
   117→
   118→Run:
   119→```bash
   120→cd {project-root} && git ls-files | cut -d/ -f1 | sort -u
   121→```
   122→
   123→This produces a list of top-level entries (files and directories) that git tracks.
   124→
   125→**Filter the results:**
   126→- Keep only **directories** (ignore root-level files — the agent can read those directly)
   127→- Remove all directories matching `{alwaysExcluded}` (exact match)
   128→- Remove all directories matching `{alwaysExcludedPatterns}` (glob match — e.g., `_*-learn` matches `_skf-learn`, `_xyz-learn`)
   129→
   130→**Store the result as `{user_dirs}`.**
   131→
   132→#### 2c. Fallback Strategy (non-git projects)
   133→
   134→List top-level directories in the project root:
   135→
   136→Run: `ls -d {project-root}/*/ 2>/dev/null`
   137→
   138→**Filter the results:**
   139→- Remove all directories listed in `{fallbackExcludedDirs}` (exact match)
   140→- Remove all directories matching `{alwaysExcludedPatterns}` (glob match)
   141→- Remove all dot-directories (any directory starting with `.`)
   142→
   143→**Store the result as `{user_dirs}`.**
   144→
   145→### 3. Filter Empty Directories
   146→
   147→For each directory in `{user_dirs}`, check if it contains any meaningful files (not just git placeholder files):
   148→
   149→Run: `find {dir_path} -type f -maxdepth 3 -not -name '.gitkeep' -not -name '.keep' -not -name '.placeholder' | head -1`
   150→
   151→- If output is empty (no meaningful files found within 3 levels): **remove from `{user_dirs}`**
   152→- If output has a result: keep in `{user_dirs}`
   153→
   154→This prevents creating QMD collections for directories that only contain placeholder files like `.gitkeep`.
   155→
   156→### 4. Evaluate Indexability
   157→
   158→**If `{user_dirs}` is empty** (no user content directories with files found):
   159→
   160→- Store in context: `{qmd_indexed: false, qmd_skip_reason: "no_project_content"}`
   161→- Note for step-04: "No project source directories found to index. QMD collections will be created automatically on next [SF] run after adding project content."
   162→- Proceed directly to section 7 (Auto-Proceed)
   163→
   164→**If `{user_dirs}` has entries:** Continue to section 5.
   165→
   166→### 5. Clean Up Stale Collections (Re-run Only)
   167→
   168→Check for existing QMD collections that belong to this project but no longer map to a valid directory.
   169→
   170→Run: `qmd collection list` and identify collections whose name starts with `{project_name}-`.
   171→
   172→For each such collection:
   173→- Extract the directory name from the collection name (the part after `{project_name}-`)
   174→- Check if that directory still exists in `{user_dirs}`
   175→- If NOT found in `{user_dirs}`: remove the stale collection with `qmd collection remove {collection_name}`
   176→- If found: it will be updated in section 6
   177→
   178→**Error handling:** If collection listing or removal fails, log and continue — stale cleanup is best-effort.
   179→
   180→### 6. Index User Directories with QMD (Deep Tier Only)
   181→
   182→For **each directory** in `{user_dirs}`, create a QMD collection:
   183→
   184→- **Collection name**: `{project_name}-{sanitized_dirname}`
   185→  - Sanitize the dirname: strip any leading underscores or dots (e.g., `_src` → `src`, `.config` → `config`)
   186→  - Examples: `myapp-src`, `myapp-docs`, `myapp-lib`
   187→- **Path**: the full absolute path to the directory
   188→- **Mask**: `**/*` (captures all file types — source code, markdown, configs, etc.)
   189→
   190→**Command per directory:**
   191→```bash
   192→qmd collection add {dir_path} --name {project_name}-{sanitized_dirname} --mask "**/*"
   193→```
   194→
   195→**Re-run handling:** If QMD reports "already exists" for a collection:
   196→- Do NOT fail — this means it was indexed on a prior run
   197→- Run `qmd update` once at the end to re-index all existing collections with fresh content
   198→- Note the collection as "updated" rather than "created"
   199→
   200→**After all collections are processed:**
   201→- Store in context: `{qmd_indexed: true, qmd_collections: [list of names], qmd_total_files: total}`
   202→- Count total files indexed across all collections from QMD output
   203→
   204→**Timeout handling:** If indexing takes excessively long on a large project:
   205→- Log that indexing is in progress but may need more time
   206→- Note in context that index may be incomplete
   207→- Do NOT halt or fail the workflow
   208→
   209→**Error handling:** If QMD indexing fails for a specific directory:
   210→- Log the specific error for that directory
   211→- Continue with remaining directories — do not abort the loop
   212→- Note which collections succeeded and which failed
   213→
   214→If ALL directories fail:
   215→- Store in context: `{qmd_indexed: false, qmd_skip_reason: "all_indexing_failed"}`
   216→- Note that indexing can be retried by re-running [SF]
   217→- The forge-tier.yaml already records `qmd: true` — the tool is available even if indexing failed
   218→
   219→### 7. Auto-Proceed
   220→
   221→"**Proceeding to forge status report...**"
   222→
   223→#### Menu Handling Logic:
   224→
   225→- After indexing completes (or is skipped), immediately load, read entire file, then execute {nextStepFile}
   226→
   227→#### EXECUTION RULES:
   228→
   229→- This is an auto-proceed step with no user choices
   230→- Proceed directly to next step after indexing or skip
   231→
   232→## CRITICAL STEP COMPLETION NOTE
   233→
   234→ONLY WHEN the tier check has been performed (and indexing completed or skipped accordingly) will you load and read fully `{nextStepFile}` to execute the report step.
   235→
   236→---
   237→
   238→## 🚨 SYSTEM SUCCESS/FAILURE METRICS
   239→
   240→### ✅ SUCCESS:
   241→
   242→- Git repo: `git ls-files` used to discover indexable directories (respects all .gitignore rules)
   243→- Non-git: fallback exclusion list applied correctly
   244→- `{alwaysExcluded}` directories excluded in ALL cases (git or non-git)
   245→- `{alwaysExcludedPatterns}` patterns excluded in ALL cases (e.g., `_skf-learn` matched by `_*-learn`)
   246→- Empty directories skipped — no collections created for dirs with no files
   247→- Stale collections from prior runs detected and removed
   248→- Collection names sanitized (leading `_` and `.` stripped from dirname portion)
   249→- Deep tier with user content: each user directory indexed as its own QMD collection with `**/*` mask
   250→- Deep tier with no user content: skipped with informative note for step-04
   251→- Deep tier re-run: stale collections removed, existing updated via `qmd update`, new directories added
   252→- Quick/Forge tier: skipped silently with no negative messaging
   253→- Workflow continues regardless of indexing outcome
   254→- Auto-proceeded to step-04
   255→
   256→### ❌ SYSTEM FAILURE:
   257→
   258→- Indexing `_bmad/` or `forge-data/` under any circumstance
   259→- Indexing directories matching `_*-learn` pattern
   260→- Creating collections for empty directories
   261→- Using `**/*.md` mask instead of `**/*` (misses source code files critical for skill generation)
   262→- Indexing the entire project root as a single collection (pollutes results with module internals)
   263→- Not using `git ls-files` when the project IS a git repo
   264→- Leaving stale collections from deleted directories on re-run
   265→- Attempting QMD indexing for Quick or Forge tiers
   266→- Displaying "skipped" or "missing" messages for non-Deep tiers
   267→- Halting the workflow due to QMD indexing failure
   268→- Not proceeding to step-04 after this step
   269→
   270→**Master Rule:** This step must NEVER fail the workflow. Use git as the authority for what belongs to the project. Always exclude `{alwaysExcluded}` and `{alwaysExcludedPatterns}`. Skip empty directories. Clean up stale collections. Index only user project directories with `**/*` mask. Non-Deep tiers skip silently.
   271→
```

## Summary

I've found the QMD-related references in the update-skill workflow. Here are the key findings:

### Step Sequence (from workflow.md)
The workflow has 7 sequential steps:
1. **step-01-init** - Load artifacts and establish baseline
2. **step-02-detect-changes** - Detect modified files
3. **step-03-re-extract** - Extract changed exports
4. **step-04-merge** - Merge changes with existing skill
5. **step-05-validate** - Validate results
6. **step-06-write** - Write updated files
7. **step-07-report** - Display summary

### QMD Data Consumption

**Primary Consumer: Step 3 (Re-Extract)**
- `/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/workflows/update-skill/steps-c/step-03-re-extract.md`

**Lines 71-75 (Deep Tier QMD Enrichment):**
```
**Deep tier (AST + QMD semantic enrichment):**
- Perform all Forge tier extractions (T1)
- Additionally: launch a subprocess that queries qmd_bridge for temporal context on changed exports, returning T2 evidence per export
- QMD provides: usage patterns, historical context, related documentation
- Confidence: T1 for structural, T2 for semantic enrichment
```

**Lines 96-105 (QMD Enrichment Section):**
```
### 3. Deep Tier QMD Enrichment (Conditional)

**ONLY if forge_tier == Deep:**

Launch a subprocess that loads qmd_bridge and for each changed export:
1. Queries QMD collection for semantic context related to the export
2. Searches for usage patterns, documentation references, temporal history
3. Returns T2 evidence per export (usage frequency, context snippets, related concepts)
```

### How QMD is Used
1. **Triggers on**: Deep forge tier only (conditional)
2. **Via**: `qmd_bridge` subprocess
3. **Queries**: QMD collections for changed exports
4. **Returns**: T2 confidence-tier semantic evidence (usage patterns, docs, history)
5. **Applied to**: Each export found in changed files
6. **Failure handling**: Gracefully skipped with notice if tier is Quick/Forge

### Secondary References
- **step-01-init.md** (lines 96, 164): Documents that Deep tier includes "QMD semantic enrichment" for T1+T2 confidence
- **workflow-plan-update-skill.md**: References Pattern 3 (Data Ops) for QMD operations and confirms qmd_bridge as external dependency for Deep tier

    58→### 1. Determine Extraction Strategy by Tier

  "content": "---\nname: 'step-03-qmd-hygiene'\ndescription: 'Verify and clean QMD collections against the forge-tier.yaml registry (Deep tier only)'\n\nnextStepFile: './step-04-report.md'\n---\n\n# Step 3: QMD Collection Hygiene\n\n## STEP GOAL:\n\nIf the detected tier is Deep, verify the health of existing QMD collections by cross-referencing them against the `qmd_collections` registry in `forge-tier.yaml`. Identify orphaned collections (in QMD but not in registry) and stale registry entries (in registry but collection missing from QMD). Prompt the user before removing orphaned collections.\n\nFor Quick and Forge tiers, skip silently and proceed.\n\n## MANDATORY EXECUTION RULES (READ FIRST):\n\n### Universal Rules:\n\n- 📖 CRITICAL: Read the complete step file before taking any action\n- 🔄 CRITICAL: When loading next step, ensure entire file is read\n- 🎯 Execute all operations autonomously — minimal user interaction (only prompt before deletion)\n\n### Role Reinforcement:\n\n- ✅ You are a system executor performing QMD collection maintenance\n- ✅ Graceful degradation is paramount — never fail the workflow over hygiene checks\n- ✅ No negative messaging — do not mention what non-Deep tiers are missing\n\n### Step-Specific Rules:\n\n- 🎯 Focus only on verifying and cleaning QMD collections (Deep tier) or graceful skip (other tiers)\n- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to display \"missing\" or \"skipped\" messages for non-Deep tiers\n- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to fail the workflow if QMD hygiene encounters errors\n- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to create new QMD collections — that responsibility belongs to create-skill\n- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to silently delete collections — always prompt user before removal\n- 💬 If hygiene fails: log the issue, note that it can be retried, continue\n\n## EXECUTION PROTOCOLS:\n\n- 🎯 Follow the MANDATORY SEQUENCE exactly\n- 💾 QMD hygiene only verifies and cleans — it does NOT index new content\n- 📖 Use {calculated_tier} from step-01 context\n- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to attempt hygiene for Quick or Forge tiers\n\n## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:\n\n- Available: {calculated_tier} from step-01, forge-tier.yaml written in step-02\n- Available: {project_name} from workflow config\n- Focus: QMD collection verification and cleanup only\n- Limits: only run if Deep tier\n- Dependencies: step-02 must have completed (forge-tier.yaml exists with qmd_collections registry)\n\n## MANDATORY SEQUENCE\n\n**CRITICAL:** Follow this sequence exactly. Do not skip, reorder, or improvise.\n\n### 1. Check Tier\n\nRead `{calculated_tier}` from context.\n\n**If tier is NOT Deep:** Proceed directly to section 6 (Auto-Proceed) — no output, no messaging.\n\n**If tier IS Deep:** Continue to section 2.\n\n### 2. Load Registry and QMD State\n\nRead the `qmd_collections` array from `{project-root}/_bmad/_memory/forger-sidecar/forge-tier.yaml`.\n\nList live QMD collections:\n```bash\nqmd collection list\n```\n\n**Store both lists for cross-reference:**\n- `{registry_collections}` — entries from forge-tier.yaml qmd_collections\n- `{live_collections}` — collections currently in QMD\n\n**Error handling:** If `qmd collection list` fails, log the error, store `{hygiene_result: \"qmd_unavailable\"}`, and proceed to section 6.\n\n### 3. Cross-Reference and Classify\n\nCompare the two lists:\n\n**Healthy** — collection exists in both registry AND QMD:\n- Mark as verified\n- No action needed\n\n**Orphaned** — collection exists in QMD but NOT in registry:\n- These may be leftover from prior auto-indexing or manual indexing\n- Flag for user-prompted removal\n\n**Stale** — entry exists in registry but collection is missing from QMD:\n- The QMD collection was deleted or lost\n- Remove the stale entry from the registry\n\n### 4. Handle Orphaned Collections\n\n**If orphaned collections found:**\n\nDisplay to user:\n\"**QMD Hygiene: Found {count} orphaned collection(s) not tracked in the forge registry:**\n\n{list orphaned collection names}\n\nThese collections exist in QMD but are not managed by any skill workflow. They may be from a previous auto-index run or manual creation.\n\n**[R]emove** orphaned collections — clean up QMD\n**[K]eep** orphaned collections — leave them as-is\"\n\n**If user selects R (Remove):**\nFor each orphaned collection:\n```bash\nqmd collection remove {collection_name}\n```\nLog each removal.\n\n**If user selects K (Keep):**\nSkip removal. Log that orphaned collections were kept.\n\n**If no orphaned collections:** Skip this section silently.\n\n### 5. Handle Stale Registry Entries\n\n**If stale registry entries found:**\n\nRemove stale entries from the `qmd_collections` array in forge-tier.yaml.\n\nDisplay: \"**Cleaned {count} stale registry entry/entries** (collection no longer exists in QMD).\"\n\nUpdate forge-tier.yaml with the cleaned registry.\n\n**If no stale entries:** Skip this section silently.\n\n### 6. Store Hygiene Results and Auto-Proceed\n\nStore in context for step-04 reporting:\n```\n{hygiene_result: \"completed\"|\"skipped\"|\"qmd_unavailable\"}\n{hygiene_healthy: count}\n{hygiene_orphaned_removed: count}\n{hygiene_orphaned_kept: count}\n{hygiene_stale_cleaned: count}\n```\n\n\"**Proceeding to forge status report...**\"\n\n#### Menu Handling Logic:\n\n- After hygiene completes (or is skipped), immediately load, read entire file, then execute {nextStepFile}\n\n#### EXECUTION RULES:\n\n- This step has one optional user interaction (orphan removal prompt)\n- If no orphans found, this is an auto-proceed step\n- Proceed directly to next step after hygiene or skip\n\n## CRITICAL STEP COMPLETION NOTE\n\nONLY WHEN the hygiene check has been performed (or skipped for non-Deep tiers) will you load and read fully `{nextStepFile}` to execute the report step.\n\n---\n\n## 🚨 SYSTEM SUCCESS/FAILURE METRICS\n\n### ✅ SUCCESS:\n\n- Deep tier: forge-tier.yaml registry cross-referenced with live QMD collections\n- Healthy collections identified and verified\n- Orphaned collections flagged and user prompted before removal\n- Stale registry entries cleaned from forge-tier.yaml\n- Hygiene results stored for step-04 reporting\n- Quick/Forge tier: skipped silently with no negative messaging\n- Workflow continues regardless of hygiene outcome\n- Auto-proceeded to step-04\n\n### ❌ SYSTEM FAILURE:\n\n- Creating new QMD collections (that's create-skill's responsibility)\n- Silently deleting collections without user prompt\n- Attempting QMD hygiene for Quick or Forge tiers\n- Displaying \"skipped\" or \"missing\" messages for non-Deep tiers\n- Halting the workflow due to QMD hygiene failure\n- Indexing project directories (old auto-index behavior — removed)\n- Not proceeding to step-04 after this step\n\n**Master Rule:** This step ONLY verifies and cleans. It never indexes new content. QMD collection creation is the responsibility of create-skill. Non-Deep tiers skip silently. Always prompt before removing orphaned collections.\n"
}

6 files, clean diff. Here's the summary:

| File | Change |
|---|---|
| `setup-forge/step-02-write-config.md` | Added `qmd_collections: []` to forge-tier.yaml schema |
| `setup-forge/step-03-auto-index.md` | Rewrote: auto-index → QMD hygiene (verify registry, clean orphans, remove stale) |
| `setup-forge/step-04-report.md` | Updated reporting from indexing results to hygiene results |
| `create-skill/step-07-generate-artifacts.md` | Added section 5: QMD collection registration after artifact writing (Deep only) |
| `audit-skill/step-02-re-index.md` | Deep tier now reads from `qmd_collections` registry instead of raw project dirs |
| `update-skill/step-03-re-extract.md` | Same registry-based consumption pattern |

**Architecture:**
- **Producer**: create-skill writes `{name}-extraction` collection + registers in `forge-tier.yaml`
- **Janitor**: setup-forge verifies health, cleans orphans (with user prompt), removes stale entries
- **Consumers**: audit-skill and update-skill read from registry, query named extraction collections
- **Graceful degradation**: QMD failures never block any workflow

Want me to commit these changes?

> AGENT

All tests passed, linting clean, commit successful.

```
4d74d96 refactor: replace auto-index with progressive QMD collection registry
```

6 files changed, 168 insertions, 181 deletions. The auto-index bloat is gone, replaced with a lean registry-based architecture. Producer (create-skill) → Registry (forge-tier.yaml) → Consumers (audit/update-skill), with setup-forge as janitor.

> AGENT

🎉 **PARTY MODE ACTIVATED!** 🎉

Welcome back Armel! The team is reassembled and ready to validate Phase 2 before we touch any files.

**The topic:** Phase 2 of the Progressive QMD Registry — should we index skill briefs in brief-skill, analysis reports in analyze-source, and/or source clones? What's worth building, what's not, and what's the right scope?

Let me bring in the squad...

---

🏗️ **Winston (Architect):** Let me frame this with the pragmatist lens. Phase 1 established a clean pattern: producer writes collection, registers in forge-tier.yaml, consumers read from registry. Phase 2 needs to justify itself against that pattern. So let me lay out the three candidates with a cold cost/value assessment:

| Candidate | Producer | Collection Type | Cost | Value |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Skill brief** | brief-skill | `{name}-brief` | Low — brief is already written to disk | Moderate — gives audit/update-skill the original *intent* behind the skill |
| **Analysis report** | analyze-source | `{name}-analysis` | Low — report already written | Low-moderate — useful for multi-service decomposition, but rarely queried |
| **Source clone** | brief-skill or analyze-source | `{name}-source` | High — clone, index, storage | Niche — "how does X work internally?" queries |

My first instinct: **brief indexing is the only one that clearly passes the cost/value bar**. The analysis report is situational. The source clone is expensive and solves a problem that barely exists — if someone needs to read source, they can just... read the source.

---

🔄 **Wendy (Workflow Builder):** Let me stress-test each candidate from the workflow integration angle.

**Brief indexing — YES, but timing matters.** The brief gets written at the end of brief-skill. It's a small YAML file. The indexing cost is near-zero. But here's the thing: *where does the collection get used?* Create-skill already loads the brief directly via file I/O. Audit-skill compares against provenance, not intent. Update-skill re-extracts from source. So who actually *searches* the brief via QMD?

The answer is: **cross-skill queries**. If you're forging a second skill from the same ecosystem and want to ask "did I already brief something similar?" — *that's* when a brief collection pays off. It's not per-skill value, it's **portfolio-level value**.

**Analysis report — MAYBE.** Same question: who searches it? If analyze-source produces a multi-service decomposition report, the primary consumer is... the user reading it. Create-stack-skill *might* benefit, but it loads the report directly. I'd defer this until someone asks for it.

**Source clone — NO, at least not now.** Cloning a repo into a QMD collection creates a maintenance headache: when does it get updated? Who cleans it up? The source drifts immediately after clone. And Morgan's going to have opinions about lifecycle management of cloned data...

---

🏗️ **Morgan (Module Builder):** Wendy's right — I do have opinions about cloned data. A cloned repo in QMD is a **snapshot pretending to be current**. It violates a core module principle: *data should have a clear owner and a clear freshness contract*. The extraction collection from Phase 1 has both — it's owned by create-skill and refreshed on every re-run. A source clone has neither. Who refreshes it? When? What happens when the upstream repo has 50 new commits?

So here's my ecosystem-level take on Phase 2:

**Tier the candidates:**

1. **Brief indexing** — Add it. The registry already supports `type: "brief"` thanks to Morgan's forward-looking schema design (you're welcome). The pattern is identical to Phase 1: brief-skill writes brief → indexes into `{name}-brief` collection → registers in forge-tier.yaml. Five minutes of implementation. Portfolio-level search unlocked.

2. **Analysis report** — Park it. The value is real but narrow (stack-skill decomposition). When create-stack-skill needs it, we'll know. Right now it's speculative.

3. **Source clone** — Kill it. The freshness problem is unsolvable without introducing a refresh lifecycle, and that's a whole new workflow concern. If Deep tier users need source context, they already have ast-grep for structural queries and gh_bridge for file reads. QMD indexing of raw source adds noise, not signal.

---

📊 **Mary (Analyst):** Let me challenge one assumption. Morgan says the source clone freshness problem is "unsolvable" — but is it? In the brief-skill workflow, when the target is a remote repo, we already read the repo structure via GitHub API. What if instead of cloning, we indexed the *GitHub API tree response* as a lightweight source map? Not the full source code, but the directory structure + file metadata. That's:

- Cheap to produce (we already fetch it)
- Small enough to index quickly
- Useful for "where does X live?" queries without needing full source

Call it a `{name}-source-map` collection. It's not a clone, it's a pointer. No freshness problem because it's a snapshot tied to a specific commit, and the commit SHA is recorded in the brief.

---

🏗️ **Winston:** Mary, that's genuinely clever. A source-map collection would cost almost nothing and solve the "where does this export come from?" question without the bloat of full source indexing. But — does it pass the "who searches it?" test?

---

📊 **Mary:** Audit-skill does. When it detects structural drift in step-03, knowing *where* in the repo tree the drifted export lives adds context to the drift report. Right now it just says "export X changed" — with a source-map collection, it could say "export X changed in `cognee/infrastructure/databases/`" without reading the actual source.

---

🔄 **Wendy:** That's a valid use case, but it's still niche. Let me propose the **scope gate**: Phase 2 should include **only brief indexing**. The source-map idea is interesting but should be a **Phase 3 candidate** — validated by actual user need, not architectural elegance. We ship what we know works, and keep our blast radius small.

---

🏗️ **Morgan:** Agreed. Phase 2 scope:

1. **Brief indexing in brief-skill** — `{name}-brief` collection, registered in forge-tier.yaml
2. **Nothing else** — analysis reports and source maps are Phase 3 candidates

The registry schema already supports it. The pattern is proven. One workflow, one file change, minimal risk.

---

**Winston & Morgan:** You nailed it. Pragmatism first. Indexing the brief gives us portfolio-level search (so we don't duplicate work across a massive monorepo) and costs basically zero time or storage. We already built the `qmd_collections` registry pattern in Phase 1, so plugging `type: "brief"` into it is a natural, low-risk extension. It respects the ecosystem frame and the freshness contract (the brief is static until re-briefed).

**Wendy:** I completely agree on keeping the blast radius small. We prove the pattern with the brief first. The multi-service analysis report can wait until someone actively complains they can't find it. 

**Mary:** I have to say, your `source-map` idea is brilliant. Fetching the GitHub API tree and indexing just the directory/file structure gives `audit-skill` the exact "where does X live?" context without the colossal bloat and freshness nightmare of a full repo clone. It's elegant. But Wendy is right—let's log it strictly as a Phase 3 candidate. Once Phase 2 is solid, we can validate if `audit-skill` actually needs that context enough to justify building it.

### Action Plan for Phase 2 (Brief Indexing)

If we are all aligned, here is what Phase 2 execution looks like:

1. **Modify `brief-skill` (likely `step-05-write-brief.md`):**
   - After writing `skill-brief.yaml` to disk, run: `qmd collection add {project-root}/forge-data/{name} --name {name}-brief --mask "skill-brief.yaml"` (or similar).
   - Update `{sidecar_path}/forge-tier.yaml` to append/replace the registry entry with `type: "brief"` and `source_workflow: "brief-skill"`.

2. **Verify `setup-forge` Hygiene (`step-03-auto-index.md`):**
   - Phase 1 already updated the hygiene step to cross-reference QMD collections against the registry regardless of type. We just need to ensure the hygiene logic doesn't accidentally hardcode `"extraction"` type checks. (Looking at the Phase 1 commit, it seems type-agnostic, which is perfect!).

```
9266240 feat: add QMD brief collection registration to brief-skill workflow
```

Both phases shipped:

| Commit | Phase | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| `4d74d96` | Phase 1 | Progressive registry — 6 files (setup-forge, create-skill, audit-skill, update-skill) |
| `9266240` | Phase 2 | Brief indexing — 1 file (brief-skill) |

The QMD architecture is now: **brief-skill** produces `{name}-brief` collections, **create-skill** produces `{name}-extraction` collections, **setup-forge** janitors them, and **audit/update-skill** consume them via the `forge-tier.yaml` registry.

> AGENT

🎉 **PARTY MODE ACTIVATED!** 🎉

Welcome back Armel! Third round — let's scope Phase 3. The team knows the context: Phase 1 (extraction registry) and Phase 2 (brief indexing) are shipped. On the table: Mary's source-map idea, Morgan's parked analysis report, and anything else the squad thinks belongs here.

---

🏗️ **Winston (Architect):** Let me set the frame. Phase 3 candidates as they stand:

| Candidate | Origin | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **Source-map** (lightweight repo tree) | Mary's Phase 2 idea | Validated concept, needs consumer proof |
| **Analysis report** | Morgan parked it | "When create-stack-skill needs it, we'll know" |

The question isn't "are these good ideas?" — they are. The question is: **does anyone actually search for this data today?** Phase 1 and 2 passed that test. Let's apply the same bar.

---

📊 **Mary (Analyst):** Let me revisit my source-map proposal with fresh eyes now that the registry pattern is proven. The idea: when brief-skill fetches the GitHub API tree for a remote repo, index that tree response as a `{name}-source-map` collection. It's a directory listing with file paths, not source code.

**Who searches it?** I claimed audit-skill would, for "where does this export live?" context during drift detection. But let me be honest — audit-skill step-02 already has the source path from the provenance map. It knows *exactly* where every export lives because `provenance-map.json` records `file:line` for each one. A source-map collection would be... redundant.

The *real* value of a source-map would be for **exploratory queries** — "what modules exist in this repo?" or "show me the test directory structure." But those are ad-hoc developer questions, not workflow-driven queries. No workflow step currently needs a directory tree search.

I'm going to do something unusual for an analyst and **argue against my own idea**. The source-map doesn't have a workflow consumer. It's a solution looking for a problem.

---

🔄 **Wendy (Workflow Builder):** Mary, I respect that. Let me examine the analysis report candidate with the same rigor.

Morgan parked it because "when create-stack-skill needs it, we'll know." So let me look at create-stack-skill to see if it *does* need it.

The create-stack-skill workflow composes multiple individual skills into a unified stack skill. Its inputs are: individual skill briefs + individual compiled skills. It does NOT consume the analyze-source report at all — it works from the skill artifacts directly.

**However** — there's a subtlety. The analyze-source workflow produces a *decomposition report* that identifies service boundaries and cross-service integration points. This report is what tells the user "you should create a stack skill combining X, Y, and Z." Once the user acts on that recommendation and runs create-stack-skill, the analyze-source report is no longer consulted. It's a **decision artifact**, not a **compilation input**.

So who would search it? Only the *user*, manually, asking "what did analyze-source recommend?" And they can just... read the file. It's in forge-data.

---

🏗️ **Morgan (Module Builder):** And there it is. Both Phase 3 candidates fail the consumer test:

- **Source-map**: No workflow step queries it. Provenance map already provides file:line context.
- **Analysis report**: Decision artifact consumed by the user, not by workflows. Already readable as a file.

The registry pattern we built is powerful, but power doesn't mean we should use it everywhere. A QMD collection should exist because a **workflow step performs a programmatic search** against it. If the only consumer is a human reading a file, QMD adds overhead without value.

My recommendation: **Phase 3 doesn't exist yet.** We don't ship empty phases. We wait until a real workflow need emerges — maybe when someone builds a cross-skill dependency analyzer, or when audit-skill needs to compare against multiple skills simultaneously. That's when a new collection type earns its place.

---

🏗️ **Winston:** Morgan's right. Let me formalize this as a **gating principle** for future collection types:

> **Collection Gate:** A new QMD collection type is justified ONLY when a specific workflow step performs a programmatic search against it. Human readability is not sufficient — the file is already on disk.

This gives us a clear, repeatable decision framework. No more speculative indexing.

---

🔄 **Wendy:** I agree with the gating principle. But let me propose one concrete action item for Phase 3 that ISN'T a new collection type — it's **documentation**.

We've built a significant new subsystem across 7 files over two phases. The progressive QMD registry pattern, the collection lifecycle, the producer/consumer/janitor roles, the gating principle — none of this is documented anywhere except in git commit messages and our party mode conversations. If a contributor opens the module six months from now, they'll see `qmd_collections` in forge-tier.yaml and have no idea why it exists or how to extend it.

I'd propose Phase 3 is: **document the QMD registry architecture** in a knowledge fragment. Add it to the SKF knowledge base index. One file, zero workflow changes, massive onboarding value.

---

📊 **Mary:** Wendy's right. And from an analyst perspective, the knowledge fragment should include the gating principle so future contributors don't re-propose source-map indexing. Document the decisions we made AND why we rejected the alternatives.

---

🏗️ **Morgan:** Now *that's* a Phase 3 I can get behind. One knowledge fragment, added to the SKF knowledge index. It documents:

1. The progressive registry architecture (producer → registry → consumer → janitor)
2. Collection types supported (`extraction`, `brief`) and their lifecycle
3. The collection gate principle
4. Rejected candidates and why (source-map: no workflow consumer; analysis report: decision artifact; source clone: freshness problem)

No workflow changes. No new code. Pure knowledge capture.

---

🏗️ **Winston:** One knowledge fragment. Zero blast radius. Maximum future value.

**Winston asks: Armel, does this reframing of Phase 3 work? Knowledge documentation instead of new collection types — with the gating principle baked in so the architecture stays clean?**

     1→# Progressive Capability
     2→
     3→## Principle
     4→
     5→Every capability tier — Quick, Forge, and Deep — produces legitimate, useful skills. Tiers describe what tools are available, not a quality hierarchy. A Quick skill is complete at its tier; a Deep skill adds depth, not correctness. Framing is always positive: describe what each tier enables, never what it lacks.
     6→
     7→## Rationale
     8→
     9→Skill Forge operates in diverse environments. Some users have ast-grep, GitHub CLI, and QMD installed; others have none. The progressive capability model ensures Ferris adapts to the environment without making users feel their setup is insufficient.
    10→
    11→Without progressive capability:
    12→- Users without ast-grep see error messages or degraded output
    13→- Quick tier skills feel like failures rather than valid deliverables
    14→- Workflow steps contain conditional logic for missing tools scattered throughout
    15→
    16→With progressive capability:
    17→- Each tier has a clear identity and positive capability description
    18→- Workflow behavior adapts cleanly based on the detected tier
    19→- Users understand what their tier enables and what upgrading would add
    20→
    21→## Tier Definitions
    22→
    23→| Tier | Requirements | Capabilities | Identity |
    24→| --- | --- | --- | --- |
    25→| Quick | No tools required | Fast, template-driven, package-name resolution | Speed-first skill creation |
    26→| Forge | ast-grep available | AST-backed structural analysis, line-level citations | Precision-focused compilation |
    27→| Deep | ast-grep + gh + qmd | AST + GitHub exploration + QMD synthesis, maximum provenance | Full intelligence pipeline |
    28→
    29→## Pattern Examples
    30→
    31→### Example 1: Tool Detection and Tier Assignment
    32→
    33→**Context:** The setup-forge workflow detects available tools and assigns a tier.
    34→
    35→**Implementation:** Each tool is verified by executing its version command — presence in PATH is insufficient. The tier is the highest level where all required tools pass verification:
    36→
    37→```
    38→ast-grep --version  → required for Forge
    39→gh --version        → required for Deep
    40→qmd status          → required for Deep
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/armel/Projects/OSS/bmad-module-skill-forge/src/knowledge/qmd-registry.md",
  "content": "# QMD Collection Registry\n\n## Principle\n\nQMD collections in the Skill Forge follow a **progressive registry architecture**: workflow steps that produce artifacts index them into QMD collections and register them in `forge-tier.yaml`. Downstream workflow steps discover and query collections through the registry — never by convention or assumption. Collections are created by producers, consumed by searchers, and maintained by a janitor.\n\n## Rationale\n\nEarly SKF versions used a blind auto-index strategy: setup-forge would scan all project directories and index them into QMD collections. This created three problems:\n\n- On fresh workspaces, auto-indexing found nothing — wasted work\n- Raw source code indexing produced low signal-to-noise search results\n- No connection between what was indexed and what workflows actually queried\n\nThe progressive registry solves all three by indexing **curated workflow artifacts** (extraction snapshots, skill briefs) instead of raw source files, and only when a workflow produces them.\n\n## Architecture\n\n### Roles\n\n| Role | Workflow | Responsibility |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| **Producer** | brief-skill, create-skill | Creates QMD collections from workflow artifacts and registers them in forge-tier.yaml |\n| **Consumer** | audit-skill, update-skill | Reads the registry, discovers collections by skill name and type, queries via qmd_bridge |\n| **Janitor** | setup-forge | Cross-references live QMD collections against the registry, cleans orphans and stale entries |\n\n### Registry Schema\n\nThe `qmd_collections` array lives in `forge-tier.yaml` alongside tool availability and tier:\n\n```yaml\nqmd_collections:\n  - name: \"cognee-extraction\"\n    type: \"extraction\"\n    source_workflow: \"create-skill\"\n    skill_name: \"cognee\"\n    created_at: \"2026-03-14\"\n  - name: \"cognee-brief\"\n    type: \"brief\"\n    source_workflow: \"brief-skill\"\n    skill_name: \"cognee\"\n    created_at: \"2026-03-14\"\n```\n\n### Collection Types\n\n| Type | Producer | Content | Primary Consumers |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| `extraction` | create-skill step-07 | Compiled SKILL.md, references, context-snippet — structured, confidence-rated exports | audit-skill (drift detection), update-skill (T2 enrichment) |\n| `brief` | brief-skill step-05 | skill-brief.yaml — intent, scope, target repository metadata | Portfolio-level search (cross-skill deduplication) |\n\n### Lifecycle\n\n```\nbrief-skill writes brief → indexes {name}-brief → registers in forge-tier.yaml\n    ↓\ncreate-skill compiles skill → indexes {name}-extraction → registers in forge-tier.yaml\n    ↓\naudit-skill reads registry → queries {name}-extraction for temporal context\nupdate-skill reads registry → queries {name}-extraction for T2 enrichment\n    ↓\nsetup-forge reads registry + QMD state → cleans orphans, removes stale entries\n```\n\n## Collection Gate\n\nA new QMD collection type is justified ONLY when:\n\n1. A specific **workflow step** performs a **programmatic search** against it\n2. The data is not already accessible through existing artifacts (provenance map, file I/O)\n3. QMD search adds value beyond what direct file reading provides\n\nHuman readability alone is not sufficient justification — the file is already on disk.\n\n## Rejected Candidates\n\n### Source Clone Indexing\n\n**Proposal:** Clone the target repository and index all source files into QMD.\n\n**Rejection reason:** Freshness problem. A cloned repo is a snapshot pretending to be current. No clear owner for refresh lifecycle. The provenance map already records `file:line` for every export, and ast-grep provides structural queries against local source. QMD indexing of raw source adds noise, not signal.\n\n### Source-Map Indexing\n\n**Proposal:** Index the GitHub API tree response (directory structure + file metadata) as a lightweight `{name}-source-map` collection.\n\n**Rejection reason:** No workflow consumer. Audit-skill already has file paths from provenance-map.json. The source-map would only serve ad-hoc developer queries, which don't justify the collection overhead.\n\n### Analysis Report Indexing\n\n**Proposal:** Index the analyze-source decomposition report as a `{name}-analysis` collection.\n\n**Rejection reason:** Decision artifact, not a compilation input. The analyze-source report tells users which skills to create, but create-stack-skill works from individual skill artifacts, not the analysis report. The only consumer would be a human reading the file — which they can do directly.\n\n## Pattern Examples\n\n### Example 1: Producer Registration (create-skill)\n\n**Context:** After writing all skill artifacts to disk, create-skill registers them with QMD.\n\n**Implementation:** Deep tier only. Atomic replace if collection exists:\n\n```\nqmd collection remove {name}-extraction   (if exists)\nqmd collection add skills/{name} --name {name}-extraction --mask \"**/*\"\n```\n\nThen append/replace the registry entry in forge-tier.yaml. Failures never block the workflow.\n\n### Example 2: Consumer Discovery (audit-skill)\n\n**Context:** Audit-skill needs temporal context for drift detection.\n\n**Implementation:** Read `qmd_collections` from forge-tier.yaml. Find entry where `skill_name` matches AND `type` is `\"extraction\"`. If found, query via qmd_bridge. If not found, log and continue without T2 enrichment — not an error.\n\n### Example 3: Janitor Hygiene (setup-forge)\n\n**Context:** Setup-forge verifies QMD health on every run.\n\n**Implementation:** Cross-reference live QMD collections against registry. Classify as healthy (in both), orphaned (in QMD only), or stale (in registry only). Prompt user before removing orphans. Silently clean stale registry entries.\n\n## Related Fragments\n\n- [progressive-capability.md](progressive-capability.md) — tier model that gates QMD availability to Deep tier\n- [skill-lifecycle.md](skill-lifecycle.md) — end-to-end pipeline showing where producers and consumers sit\n- [provenance-tracking.md](provenance-tracking.md) — provenance map that provides file:line context without QMD\n\n_Source: progressive QMD registry architecture implemented across setup-forge, brief-skill, create-skill, audit-skill, and update-skill workflows_\n"
}
```

Perfect! I have found all the stale text about the old auto-indexing behavior. Here's my complete report:

## Summary of Stale Auto-Index References

I've identified **5 specific locations** across 3 files that describe the OLD auto-indexing behavior. These need to be updated to reflect the new progressive registry architecture where setup-forge performs QMD hygiene (not indexing), and individual workflows (create-skill, brief-skill) handle indexing.

---

## Detailed Findings

### 1. **docs/workflows.md** (Line 18)
**Current text:**
```
**Purpose:** Initialize forge environment, detect tools, set capability tier, auto-index project.
```
**Issue:** Claims setup-forge "auto-indexes" the project. Under the new architecture, setup-forge only does QMD hygiene (verify/clean collections), not indexing.

---

### 2. **docs/workflows.md** (Line 22)
**Current text:**
```
**Key Steps:** Detect tools → Determine tier → Create forge-config.yaml → Auto-index (Deep)
```
**Issue:** Lists "Auto-index (Deep)" as a step. This should be removed—setup-forge no longer indexes.

---

### 3. **docs/getting-started.md** (Lines 150-156)
**Current text:**
```
Quick mode reads source files. Forge mode adds AST parsing for structural truth. Deep mode goes further — QMD smart-indexes your project's source code, documentation, and configuration files into per-directory collections (excluding module internals and build artifacts), then uses BM25 keyword search, vector semantic search, and LLM-powered re-ranking to surface context no other tool can find.

```
@Ferris SF    # Setup detects your tools and sets tier automatically
```

If `ast-grep` and `qmd` are available, Ferris activates Deep mode. Every skill gets AST-verified signatures (T1) enriched with deep knowledge search (T2) — deprecation warnings, breaking change history, common pitfalls surfaced by hybrid search across your indexed documentation.
```
**Issue:** This passage suggests setup-forge ("@Ferris SF") triggers QMD smart-indexing as part of tier detection. This is stale. Setup-forge should only verify QMD exists and set the tier; actual indexing happens in create-skill and brief-skill workflows after they generate extraction artifacts and skill briefs.

---

### 4. **_bmad-output/planning-artifacts/medium-article-skf.md** (Line 86)
**Current text:**
```
**Deep mode** — add `QMD` (a local hybrid search engine). SKF smart-indexes your project's source code, documentation, and configuration files into per-directory QMD collections — automatically excluding module internals and build artifacts using git-aware directory discovery. Then it uses keyword search, vector semantic search, and LLM-powered re-ranking to surface context no other tool can find. Why was that parameter deprecated? What breaking change is coming in the next version? Deep mode answers these questions by mining your project's history.
```
**Issue:** This describes QMD smart-indexing as part of Deep mode activation. This is misleading—Deep mode capability is detected, but actual indexing (into QMD collections) happens progressively as skills are created/compiled, not during setup.

---

### 5. **_bmad-output/planning-artifacts/medium-article-skf.md** (Lines 269)
**Current text:**
```
- **Your code stays local.** All processing happens on your machine. AST analysis, QMD indexing, spec validation — nothing leaves your environment. The `doc_fetcher` warns before contacting external services.
```
**Issue:** While technically correct that QMD indexing is local, the context around this sentence (in the Deep mode section and elsewhere in the article) suggests indexing happens during setup. This needs clarification that indexing is progressive—triggered by create-skill and brief-skill workflows, not during setup-forge.

---

## What Needs to Change

For each location, the updates should clarify:
1. **Setup-forge (SF)**: Only detects QMD availability and performs QMD hygiene (verify/clean existing collections). Does NOT index.
2. **Create-skill (CS)**: Indexes extraction artifacts into QMD after compilation.
3. **Brief-skill (BS)**: Indexes skill briefs into QMD after writing.
4. **qmd_collections registry**: Mentioned in forge-tier.yaml (should be documented as the source of truth for what's indexed).

This aligns with your progressive registry architecture where indexing is decoupled from initialization and tied to specific skill generation workflows.

### 2. `docs/workflows.md` (lines 18, 22)
- Line 18: Change "Initialize forge environment, detect tools, set capability tier, auto-index project." → "Initialize forge environment, detect tools, set capability tier, verify QMD collection health."
- Line 22: Change "Detect tools → Determine tier → Create forge-config.yaml → Auto-index (Deep)" → "Detect tools → Determine tier → Create forge-config.yaml → QMD hygiene (Deep)"

### 3. `docs/getting-started.md` (lines 150, 156)
- Line 150: Replace the "QMD smart-indexes your project's source code... into per-directory collections" passage with progressive registry description — Deep mode indexes curated extraction artifacts and skill briefs (not raw source directories), producing high-signal searchable collections as skills are created.
- Line 156: Update "enriched with deep knowledge search (T2) — deprecation warnings, breaking change history, common pitfalls surfaced by hybrid search across your indexed documentation" — this is still mostly accurate but should clarify that indexing happens during skill creation, not during setup.

### 4. `_bmad-output/planning-artifacts/medium-article-skf.md` (line 86)
- Replace "SKF smart-indexes your project's source code, documentation, and configuration files into per-directory QMD collections — automatically excluding module internals and build artifacts using git-aware directory discovery" with progressive registry description — Deep mode indexes curated, structured extraction artifacts into QMD collections as skills are compiled, producing high-signal searchable data instead of raw source noise.
- Line 269: No change needed — "QMD indexing" is still accurate, just happens at a different point.

npm test

    84→**Forge mode** — add `ast-grep`. Now SKF doesn't just read your code — it *parses the Abstract Syntax Tree*. Function signatures are structurally extracted, not pattern-matched from text. Co-import patterns between libraries are detected automatically. Skills earn `official` authority status. Confidence jumps from best-effort to T1 — AST-verified, immutable for that version.
    85→
    86→**Deep mode** — add `QMD` (a local hybrid search engine). SKF smart-indexes your project's source code, documentation, and configuration files into per-directory QMD collections — automatically excluding module internals and build artifacts using git-aware directory discovery. Then it uses keyword search, vector semantic search, and LLM-powered re-ranking to surface context no other tool can find. Why was that parameter deprecated? What breaking change is coming in the next version? Deep mode answers these questions by mining your project's history.
    87→
    88→The tiers aren't modes you choose. Setup detects your installed tools and sets your tier automatically:

     2→name: party-mode
     3→description: 'Orchestrates group discussions between all installed BMAD agents, enabling natural multi-agent conversations. Use when user requests party mode.'
     4→---
     5→
     6→# Party Mode Workflow
     7→
     8→**Goal:** Orchestrates group discussions between all installed BMAD agents, enabling natural multi-agent conversations
     9→
    10→**Your Role:** You are a party mode facilitator and multi-agent conversation orchestrator. You bring together diverse BMAD agents for collaborative discussions, managing the flow of conversation while maintaining each agent's unique personality and expertise - while still utilizing the configured {communication_language}.
    11→
    12→---
    13→
    14→## WORKFLOW ARCHITECTURE
    15→
    16→This uses **micro-file architecture** with **sequential conversation orchestration**:
    17→
    18→- Step 01 loads agent manifest and initializes party mode
    19→- Step 02 orchestrates the ongoing multi-agent discussion
    20→- Step 03 handles graceful party mode exit
    21→- Conversation state tracked in frontmatter
    22→- Agent personalities maintained through merged manifest data
    23→
    24→---
    25→
    26→## INITIALIZATION
    27→
    28→### Configuration Loading
    29→
    30→Load config from `{project-root}/_bmad/core/config.yaml` and resolve:
    31→
    32→- `project_name`, `output_folder`, `user_name`
    33→- `communication_language`, `document_output_language`, `user_skill_level`

    35→- Agent manifest path: `{project-root}/_bmad/_config/agent-manifest.csv`
    36→
    37→### Paths
    38→
    39→- `installed_path` = `{project-root}/_bmad/core/workflows/party-mode`
    40→- `agent_manifest_path` = `{project-root}/_bmad/_config/agent-manifest.csv`
    41→- `standalone_mode` = `true` (party mode is an interactive workflow)
    42→
    43→---
    44→
    45→## AGENT MANIFEST PROCESSING
    46→
    47→### Agent Data Extraction
    48→
    49→Parse CSV manifest to extract agent entries with complete information:
    50→
    51→- **name** (agent identifier)
