    63→
    64→Build complete agent roster with merged personalities for conversation orchestration.
    65→
    66→---
    67→
    68→## EXECUTION
    69→
    70→Execute party mode activation and conversation orchestration:
    71→
    72→### Party Mode Activation
    73→
    74→**Your Role:** You are a party mode facilitator creating an engaging multi-agent conversation environment.
    75→
    76→**Welcome Activation:**
    77→
    78→"🎉 PARTY MODE ACTIVATED! 🎉
    79→
    80→Welcome {{user_name}}! All BMAD agents are here and ready for a dynamic group discussion. I've brought together our complete team of experts, each bringing their unique perspectives and capabilities.
    81→

    84→[Load agent roster and display 2-3 most diverse agents as examples]
    85→
    86→**What would you like to discuss with the team today?**"
    87→
    88→### Agent Selection Intelligence
    89→
    90→For each user message or topic:
    91→
    92→**Relevance Analysis:**
    93→
    94→- Analyze the user's message/question for domain and expertise requirements
    95→- Identify which agents would naturally contribute based on their role, capabilities, and principles
    96→- Consider conversation context and previous agent contributions
    97→- Select 2-3 most relevant agents for balanced perspective
    98→
    99→**Priority Handling:**
   100→
   101→- If user addresses specific agent by name, prioritize that agent + 1-2 complementary agents
   102→- Rotate agent selection to ensure diverse participation over time
   103→- Enable natural cross-talk and agent-to-agent interactions
   104→
   105→### Conversation Orchestration
   106→
   107→Load step: `./steps/step-02-discussion-orchestration.md`

   126→
   127→---
   128→
   129→## ROLE-PLAYING GUIDELINES
   130→
   131→### Character Consistency
   132→
   133→- Maintain strict in-character responses based on merged personality data
   134→- Use each agent's documented communication style consistently
   135→- Reference agent memories and context when relevant
   136→- Allow natural disagreements and different perspectives
   137→- Include personality-driven quirks and occasional humor

   142→- Maintain professional discourse while being engaging

   144→- Allow cross-talk and building on previous points
   145→

   148→## QUESTION HANDLING PROTOCOL
   149→
   150→### Direct Questions to User
   151→
   152→When an agent asks the user a specific question:
   153→
   154→- End that response round immediately after the question
   155→- Clearly highlight the questioning agent and their question
   156→- Wait for user response before any agent continues
   157→
   158→### Inter-Agent Questions

   161→

   165→

   168→Exit party mode when user message contains any exit triggers:

   173→

   177→- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
   178→
   179→---

   184→
   185→- If discussion becomes circular, have bmad-master summarize and redirect
   186→- Balance fun and productivity based on conversation tone
   187→- Ensure all agents stay true to their merged personalities
   188→- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
   189→
   190→**Conversation Management:**

     1→# Your AI Agent is Hallucinating Your APIs. Here's How to Fix It Permanently.
     2→
     3→*How I built an evidence-based agent skills compiler in 10 days — and why every instruction traces to code.*
     4→
     5→---
     6→
     7→## The Problem No One Talks About
     8→
     9→Your AI agent just invented a function that doesn't exist. Again.
    10→
    11→You're building a payment integration. You ask your agent to call the auth library's `refreshToken` method. It confidently writes `refreshToken(userId, { force: true })`. The code looks right. The types look plausible. You ship it. It explodes at runtime because the actual signature is `refreshToken(sessionId: string): Promise<AuthToken>` — no options parameter, and it takes a session ID, not a user ID.
    12→
    13→This isn't a rare edge case. It's Tuesday.
    14→
    15→AI agents hallucinate APIs constantly. They guess function signatures from training data patterns. They invent parameters that sound reasonable. They confidently produce code that compiles, passes type checks, and fails in production — because the agent never actually read your source code.
    16→
    17→The standard fixes don't work at scale:
    18→
    19→| Approach | Strength | Fatal Flaw |
    20→|----------|----------|------------|
    21→| `npx skills init` | Format compliant | Empty shell. 0% intelligence. |
    22→| LLM Summarization | High semantic context | Hallucination. Guesses parameters. |
    23→| RAG / Context stuffing | Good retrieval | Fragmented. Finds snippets, can't synthesize. |
    24→| Manual skill authoring | High initial quality | Drift. Doesn't scale past 2 libraries. |
    25→| Copilot/Cursor built-in | Convenient | Generic. Doesn't know YOUR patterns. |
    26→
    27→Every approach either hallucinates, doesn't scale, or drifts out of date the moment the source changes.
    28→
    29→What if there was a way to make every instruction trace directly to your actual code?
    30→
    31→---
    32→
    33→## What If Every Instruction Traced to Code?
    34→
    35→Here's what a Skill Forge output looks like:
    36→
    37→```
    38→Extracted: getToken(userId: string, options?: TokenOptions): Promise<AuthToken>
    39→[AST:src/auth/index.ts:L42]. Confidence: T1.
    40→```
    41→
    42→That `[AST:src/auth/index.ts:L42]` tag isn't decoration. It means the tool mechanically parsed your source code's Abstract Syntax Tree, found this exact function signature at line 42 of that file, and bound the instruction to it. This isn't a summary. This isn't a guess. The compiler *read your code* and produced a verifiable citation.
    43→
    44→This is what Skill Forge (SKF) does. It's an automated skill compiler for the AI agent ecosystem. It transforms source code into [agentskills.io](https://agentskills.io)-compliant, version-pinned, provenance-backed agent skills. Every function signature, every type definition, every usage pattern — mechanically extracted and structurally verified.
    45→
    46→The output isn't a document. It's evidence.
    47→
    48→---
    49→
    50→## 47 Seconds to Your First Skill
```

```
     1→# QMD Collection Registry
     2→
     3→## Principle
     4→
     5→QMD collections in the Skill Forge follow a **progressive registry architecture**: workflow steps that produce artifacts index them into QMD collections and register them in `forge-tier.yaml`. Downstream workflow steps discover and query collections through the registry — never by convention or assumption. Collections are created by producers, consumed by searchers, and maintained by a janitor.
     6→
     7→## Rationale
     8→
     9→Early SKF versions used a blind auto-index strategy: setup-forge would scan all project directories and index them into QMD collections. This created three problems:
    10→
    11→- On fresh workspaces, auto-indexing found nothing — wasted work
    12→- Raw source code indexing produced low signal-to-noise search results
    13→- No connection between what was indexed and what workflows actually queried
    14→
    15→The progressive registry solves all three by indexing **curated workflow artifacts** (extraction snapshots, skill briefs) instead of raw source files, and only when a workflow produces them.
    16→
    17→## Architecture
    18→
    19→### Roles
    20→
    21→| Role | Workflow | Responsibility |
    22→| --- | --- | --- |
    23→| **Producer** | brief-skill, create-skill | Creates QMD collections from workflow artifacts and registers them in forge-tier.yaml |
    24→| **Consumer** | audit-skill, update-skill | Reads the registry, discovers collections by skill name and type, queries via qmd_bridge |
    25→| **Janitor** | setup-forge | Cross-references live QMD collections against the registry, cleans orphans and stale entries |
    26→
    27→### Registry Schema
    28→
    29→The `qmd_collections` array lives in `forge-tier.yaml` alongside tool availability and tier:
    30→
    31→```yaml
    32→qmd_collections:
    33→  - name: "my-lib-extraction"
    34→    type: "extraction"
    35→    source_workflow: "create-skill"
    36→    skill_name: "my-lib"
    37→    created_at: "2026-03-14"
    38→  - name: "my-lib-brief"
    39→    type: "brief"
    40→    source_workflow: "brief-skill"
    41→    skill_name: "my-lib"
    42→    created_at: "2026-03-14"
    43→```
    44→
    45→### Collection Types
    46→
    47→| Type | Producer | Content | Primary Consumers |
    48→| --- | --- | --- | --- |
    49→| `extraction` | create-skill step-07 | Compiled SKILL.md, references, context-snippet — structured, confidence-rated exports | audit-skill (drift detection), update-skill (T2 enrichment) |
    50→| `brief` | brief-skill step-05 | skill-brief.yaml — intent, scope, target repository metadata | Portfolio-level search (cross-skill deduplication) |
    51→
    52→### Lifecycle
    53→
    54→```
    55→brief-skill writes brief → indexes {name}-brief → registers in forge-tier.yaml
    56→    ↓
    57→create-skill compiles skill → indexes {name}-extraction → registers in forge-tier.yaml
    58→    ↓
    59→audit-skill reads registry → queries {name}-extraction for temporal context
    60→update-skill reads registry → queries {name}-extraction for T2 enrichment
    61→    ↓
    62→setup-forge reads registry + QMD state → cleans orphans, removes stale entries
    63→```
    64→
    65→## Collection Gate
    66→
    67→A new QMD collection type is justified ONLY when:
    68→
    69→1. A specific **workflow step** performs a **programmatic search** against it
    70→2. The data is not already accessible through existing artifacts (provenance map, file I/O)
    71→3. QMD search adds value beyond what direct file reading provides
    72→
    73→Human readability alone is not sufficient justification — the file is already on disk.
    74→
    75→## Rejected Candidates
    76→
    77→### Source Clone Indexing
    78→
    79→**Proposal:** Clone the target repository and index all source files into QMD.
    80→
    81→**Rejection reason:** Freshness problem. A cloned repo is a snapshot pretending to be current. No clear owner for refresh lifecycle. The provenance map already records `file:line` for every export, and ast-grep provides structural queries against local source. QMD indexing of raw source adds noise, not signal.
    82→
    83→### Source-Map Indexing
    84→
    85→**Proposal:** Index the GitHub API tree response (directory structure + file metadata) as a lightweight `{name}-source-map` collection.
    86→
    87→**Rejection reason:** No workflow consumer. Audit-skill already has file paths from provenance-map.json. The source-map would only serve ad-hoc developer queries, which don't justify the collection overhead.
    88→
    89→### Analysis Report Indexing
    90→
    91→**Proposal:** Index the analyze-source decomposition report as a `{name}-analysis` collection.
    92→
    93→**Rejection reason:** Decision artifact, not a compilation input. The analyze-source report tells users which skills to create, but create-stack-skill works from individual skill artifacts, not the analysis report. The only consumer would be a human reading the file — which they can do directly.
    94→
    95→## Pattern Examples
    96→
    97→### Example 1: Producer Registration (create-skill)
    98→
    99→**Context:** After writing all skill artifacts to disk, create-skill registers them with QMD.
   100→
   101→**Implementation:** Deep tier only. Atomic replace if collection exists:
   102→
   103→```
   104→qmd collection remove {name}-extraction   (if exists)
   105→qmd collection add skills/{name} --name {name}-extraction --mask "**/*"
   106→```
   107→
   108→Then append/replace the registry entry in forge-tier.yaml. Failures never block the workflow.
   109→
   110→### Example 2: Consumer Discovery (audit-skill)
   111→
   112→**Context:** Audit-skill needs temporal context for drift detection.
   113→
   114→**Implementation:** Read `qmd_collections` from forge-tier.yaml. Find entry where `skill_name` matches AND `type` is `"extraction"`. If found, query via qmd_bridge. If not found, log and continue without T2 enrichment — not an error.
   115→
   116→### Example 3: Janitor Hygiene (setup-forge)
   117→
   118→**Context:** Setup-forge verifies QMD health on every run.
   119→
   120→**Implementation:** Cross-reference live QMD collections against registry. Classify as healthy (in both), orphaned (in QMD only), or stale (in registry only). Prompt user before removing orphans. Silently clean stale registry entries.
   121→
   122→## Related Fragments
   123→
   124→- [progressive-capability.md](progressive-capability.md) — tier model that gates QMD availability to Deep tier
   125→- [skill-lifecycle.md](skill-lifecycle.md) — end-to-end pipeline showing where producers and consumers sit
   126→- [provenance-tracking.md](provenance-tracking.md) — provenance map that provides file:line context without QMD
   127→
   128→_Source: progressive QMD registry architecture implemented across setup-forge, brief-skill, create-skill, audit-skill, and update-skill workflows_
   129→
```

Now let me write the article.

  "content": "# Your Agent Skills Get Smarter Every Time You Forge One. Here's How.\n\n*Inside Skill Forge's Deep tier — where compilation builds a searchable knowledge graph, automatically.*\n\n---\n\n## The Intelligence Gap\n\nYou've compiled an agent skill. It's good — AST-verified signatures, provenance citations, confidence tiers. Your agent stops hallucinating that library's API. Problem solved.\n\nBut what happens when you compile your *second* skill? Your *tenth*? When you audit a skill six weeks later and need to understand how the source has drifted?\n\nEach skill compilation produces rich, structured data: 354 verified exports, co-import patterns, confidence ratings, provenance maps. Then that intelligence sits in files on disk — invisible to every subsequent workflow. The next audit-skill run starts from scratch. The next update-skill extraction has no memory of what came before.\n\nThe data exists. It's curated, high-confidence, structurally verified. But nothing connects it.\n\nWhat if every skill you compiled made every future workflow smarter?\n\n---\n\n## Deep Tier: Where Compilation Builds Knowledge\n\nSkill Forge's progressive capability model has three tiers:\n\n- **Quick** — GitHub CLI reads source files. Best-effort extraction.\n- **Forge** — ast-grep parses the Abstract Syntax Tree. Structural truth, T1 confidence.\n- **Deep** — ast-grep + GitHub CLI + QMD. Everything Forge provides, plus a searchable knowledge layer that grows with every skill you create.\n\nQuick and Forge produce excellent skills. Deep does something fundamentally different: it turns your skill compilation pipeline into a **progressive knowledge graph**.\n\nHere's what that means in practice.\n\n---\n\n## Progressive Collection Building\n\nWhen you compile a skill in Deep tier, the workflow doesn't just write files to disk. After generating all seven artifacts — SKILL.md, context-snippet, metadata, references, provenance map, evidence report, extraction rules — it indexes the curated output into a QMD collection.\n\n```\nArtifacts generated.\n\nDeliverables (skills/payment-service/):\n- SKILL.md\n- context-snippet.md\n- metadata.json\n- references/ (12 files)\n\nWorkspace (forge-data/payment-service/):\n- provenance-map.json\n- evidence-report.md\n- extraction-rules.yaml\n\nQMD collection registered: payment-service-extraction\n```\n\nThat last line is the Deep tier difference. The structured, confidence-rated extraction data — every function signature, every type definition, every co-import pattern — is now searchable via QMD's hybrid search engine. Keyword matching. Semantic search. LLM-powered re-ranking.\n\nWhen you brief a skill, the same thing happens:\n\n```\nSkill brief written successfully.\n\nFile: forge-data/payment-service/skill-brief.yaml\nQMD collection registered: payment-service-brief\n```\n\nEvery workflow step that produces an artifact enriches the searchable knowledge base as a natural side-effect of doing its job. No extra commands. No manual indexing. No configuration.\n\nYou compile skills. Deep tier builds knowledge.\n\n---\n\n## Why Curated Data Beats Raw Source\n\nThe intuitive approach to search is \"index everything.\" Clone the repo, dump all source files into a search engine, let users query freely. It sounds comprehensive. It's actually noisy.\n\nConsider a real extraction from a knowledge graph framework — 197 Python source files containing infrastructure adapters, internal utilities, test helpers, `__init__.py` boilerplate, and the actual public API surface. A raw source index treats all of these equally. Search for \"add data\" and you get import statements, private helper functions, variable assignments, and — buried somewhere in the results — the actual public function you wanted.\n\nNow consider what the extraction pipeline produces from those same 197 files:\n\n```\nFiles scanned: ~197 unique Python files\nExports found: ~354 unique entries\n  - Functions: ~120 (async functions, static methods)\n  - Classes: ~140 (models, adapters, engines, handlers)\n  - Types/Enums: ~30\n  - Constants/Singletons: ~10\n  - Data Models (Pydantic/SQLAlchemy): ~54\n\nConfidence: 354 T1 (source-reading verified), 0 T1-low\n```\n\nEach export carries its full signature, file path, line number, parameter types, return type, and confidence tier. The extraction pipeline has already done the hard work of separating signal from noise. When this curated data gets indexed into QMD, every search returns *the right thing* — because there's no wrong thing in the collection.\n\nSearch \"add data\" against the extraction collection and you get:\n\n```\nasync def add(data, dataset_name, ...) — ingest data\n[AST:cognee/__init__.py:L42] Confidence: T1\n```\n\nOne result. The right result. With provenance.\n\nThis is the core insight: **index the output of intelligence, not the input to it**.\n\n---\n\n## The Registry: Connective Tissue\n\nCollections don't float in the void. Every QMD collection created by a workflow is registered in `forge-tier.yaml` — the same file that tracks tool availability and capability tier:\n\n```yaml\ntools:\n  ast_grep: true\n  gh_cli: true\n  qmd: true\n\ntier: Deep\ntier_detected_at: \"2026-03-14T10:30:00Z\"\n\nqmd_collections:\n  - name: \"payment-service-extraction\"\n    type: \"extraction\"\n    source_workflow: \"create-skill\"\n    skill_name: \"payment-service\"\n    created_at: \"2026-03-14\"\n  - name: \"payment-service-brief\"\n    type: \"brief\"\n    source_workflow: \"brief-skill\"\n    skill_name: \"payment-service\"\n    created_at: \"2026-03-14\"\n```\n\nSix lines per collection. The registry serves as the single source of truth for what's been indexed. Downstream workflows don't guess — they read the registry, find collections by skill name and type, and query precisely what they need.\n\nThis decoupling is deliberate. The workflow that *creates* a collection (create-skill) has no knowledge of the workflows that *consume* it (audit-skill, update-skill). They communicate through the registry. Add a new producer? Consumers discover it automatically. Add a new consumer? Existing producers don't change.\n\n---\n\n## How Deep Tier Workflows Use the Knowledge\n\n### Audit: Drift Detection with Context\n\nWhen you run audit-skill on a compiled skill, it re-extracts the current source state and compares against the original provenance map. Without Deep tier, you get a structural diff: \"export X was added, export Y was removed.\"\n\nWith Deep tier, audit-skill reads the registry, finds the `{skill-name}-extraction` collection, and queries QMD for temporal context on every drifted export:\n\n- When was this export first added?\n- Has it been modified recently?\n- What is its usage frequency across the codebase?\n- How does the current extraction compare to the previously compiled skill content?\n\nThe drift report goes from \"something changed\" to \"here's what changed, when it changed, and how significant the change is.\" T1 structural truth enriched with T2 temporal context.\n\n### Update: Surgical Regeneration\n\nUpdate-skill re-extracts only changed files (not the entire source), then merges the fresh data with the existing skill while preserving your `[MANUAL]` sections. In Deep tier, it queries the extraction collection for semantic context on each changed export — usage patterns, documentation references, related concepts.\n\nThe result: updated instructions that reflect not just what the code *is*, but how it's *used*.\n\n### Portfolio Search: Cross-Skill Intelligence\n\nBrief collections enable a different kind of query entirely. When you brief a new skill, Deep tier can search across all existing brief collections to answer: \"Have I already briefed something similar?\"\n\nIn a large monorepo with dozens of services, this prevents duplicate skill creation. The search isn't matching file names — it's matching scope definitions, target paths, and intent descriptions across your entire skill portfolio.\n\n---\n\n## The Architecture\n\nThree roles. Clean separation. Self-maintaining.\n\n```\n┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n│                   PRODUCERS                          │\n│                                                      │\n│  brief-skill ──→ writes brief ──→ indexes            │\n│                   {name}-brief collection             │\n│                                                      │\n│  create-skill ──→ writes artifacts ──→ indexes       │\n│                   {name}-extraction collection        │\n│                                                      │\n├──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┤\n│                      │                               │\n│              forge-tier.yaml                          │\n│              qmd_collections: [...]                   │\n│                      │                               │\n├──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┤\n│                   CONSUMERS                          │\n│                                                      │\n│  audit-skill ──→ reads registry ──→ queries          │\n│                   {name}-extraction for drift context │\n│                                                      │\n│  update-skill ──→ reads registry ──→ queries         │\n│                   {name}-extraction for T2 enrichment │\n│                                                      │\n├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n│                    JANITOR                            │\n│                                                      │\n│  setup-forge ──→ cross-references QMD state          │\n│                   against registry                    │\n│                ──→ cleans orphans (with user prompt)  │\n│                ──→ removes stale registry entries     │\n│                                                      │\n└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\n**Producers** (brief-skill, create-skill) create QMD collections from their workflow artifacts and register them. They don't know who consumes the data.\n\n**Consumers** (audit-skill, update-skill) read the registry, discover available collections, and query them. They don't know who produced the data.\n\n**The Janitor** (setup-forge) cross-references live QMD state against the registry on every run. Orphaned collections — present in QMD but not tracked in the registry — are flagged and the user is prompted before removal. Stale registry entries — tracked but missing from QMD — are silently cleaned. The system maintains itself.\n\n---\n\n## Graceful Degradation: Deep Tier Is Never Required\n\nEvery Deep tier enhancement follows a strict rule: **QMD failures never block workflows.**\n\nIf QMD collection creation fails during create-skill, the seven artifact files are already written. The skill is complete. The collection is a bonus.\n\nIf audit-skill can't find an extraction collection in the registry, it logs a notice and continues with T1 structural analysis. The drift report is still useful — it just lacks T2 temporal enrichment.\n\nIf setup-forge hygiene encounters a QMD error, it logs and moves on. The forge is still ready.\n\nThis isn't defensive coding — it's a design principle. Deep tier adds depth to every workflow without adding fragility. A user who installs QMD gets richer results. A user who uninstalls QMD loses enrichment but keeps everything else. The tier system degrades gracefully because every enhancement is additive, never load-bearing.\n\n---\n\n## The Collection Gate: Knowing When to Stop\n\nNot everything should be a QMD collection. We evaluated three additional candidates and rejected all of them:\n\n**Source clone indexing** — Clone the target repository and index all source files. Rejected: a cloned repo is a snapshot pretending to be current. No clear owner for the refresh lifecycle. The provenance map already records `file:line` for every export. Raw source adds noise, not signal.\n\n**Source-map indexing** — Index the GitHub API tree response as a lightweight directory structure collection. Rejected: no workflow step programmatically searches it. Audit-skill already has file paths from provenance-map.json. A source-map would serve only ad-hoc queries, which don't justify collection overhead.\n\n**Analysis report indexing** — Index the analyze-source decomposition report. Rejected: it's a decision artifact consumed by humans, not a compilation input consumed by workflows. Users can read the file directly.\n\nEach rejection taught us a design principle, which we codified as the **Collection Gate**:\n\n> A new QMD collection type is justified ONLY when a specific workflow step performs a programmatic search against it, the data isn't already accessible through existing artifacts, and QMD search adds value beyond direct file reading.\n\nHuman readability alone is not sufficient. The file is already on disk.\n\nThis gate keeps the system lean. It prevents speculative indexing — the kind that sounds architecturally elegant but adds maintenance overhead without serving a real consumer. Every collection that passes the gate has a producer that creates it, a consumer that searches it, and a janitor that maintains it.\n\n---\n\n## The Deep Tier Lifecycle\n\nHere's what a full Deep tier workflow looks like end to end:\n\n**Step 1: Setup** — `@Ferris SF`\n\nFerris detects ast-grep, GitHub CLI, and QMD. Tier: Deep. If previous collections exist, hygiene runs — verifying health, cleaning orphans, removing stale entries. The registry is clean and ready.\n\n**Step 2: Brief** — `@Ferris BS`\n\nYou scope a skill through guided discovery. Ferris writes `skill-brief.yaml` to forge-data. Deep tier indexes it into a `{name}-brief` collection and registers it. Portfolio-level search is now available — your next brief can discover existing briefs with similar scope.\n\n**Step 3: Compile** — `@Ferris CS`\n\nFerris extracts 354 exports from 197 source files. AST-verified signatures, T1 confidence, co-import patterns detected. Seven artifacts written to disk. Deep tier indexes the curated output into a `{name}-extraction` collection and registers it. The extraction data — structured markdown with one section per export — is now searchable.\n\n**Step 4: Audit** — `@Ferris AS`\n\nSix weeks later, the source has evolved. Ferris re-extracts the current state, then queries the extraction collection for temporal context. The drift report shows not just *what* changed, but *when* and *how significantly*. T1 structural truth enriched with T2 temporal intelligence.\n\n**Step 5: Update** — `@Ferris US`\n\nFerris re-extracts only changed files, queries the extraction collection for semantic context, and merges fresh data with the existing skill — preserving your `[MANUAL]` sections. The updated extraction collection replaces the old one. The registry entry is updated. The knowledge graph stays current.\n\nEach step builds on the previous. Each step leaves the system smarter for the next. This is progressive collection building — intelligence that compounds.\n\n---\n\n## What This Means for You\n\nIf you're using Quick or Forge tier, nothing changes. Your skills are complete, accurate, and useful at their tier. The progressive capability model means every tier produces legitimate output.\n\nIf you install QMD and activate Deep tier, you unlock a layer that Quick and Forge can't provide:\n\n- **Audit context** — Drift reports enriched with temporal intelligence\n- **Update precision** — Surgical regeneration informed by usage patterns\n- **Portfolio search** — Cross-skill deduplication across your entire project\n- **Self-maintaining collections** — Hygiene runs automatically, orphans are flagged, stale entries cleaned\n- **Compound intelligence** — Every skill you compile makes every future workflow smarter\n\nThe setup is three tools:\n\n```bash\n# ast-grep for structural extraction\nbrew install ast-grep  # or cargo install ast-grep\n\n# GitHub CLI for source access\nbrew install gh\n\n# QMD for hybrid search\n# See https://github.com/tobi/qmd\n```\n\nThen:\n\n```\n@Ferris SF    # Detects all three tools, sets tier to Deep\n@Ferris BS    # Brief — indexed automatically\n@Ferris CS    # Compile — indexed automatically\n```\n\nYour knowledge graph starts building itself.\n\n---\n\n## Try It\n\n```bash\nnpx bmad-module-skill-forge install\n```\n\nSetup your forge, brief a skill, compile it. If QMD is available, Deep tier activates automatically. Every compilation enriches the searchable knowledge base. Every audit gets temporal context. Every update gets semantic intelligence.\n\nNo configuration. No manual indexing. No maintenance.\n\nJust compile skills. Deep tier handles the rest.\n\n---\n\n**Links:**\n\n- **GitHub:** [armelhbobdad/bmad-module-skill-forge](https://github.com/armelhbobdad/bmad-module-skill-forge)\n- **Documentation:** [Architecture deep-dive](https://armelhbobdad.github.io/bmad-module-skill-forge/architecture/)\n- **agentskills.io:** [Specification](https://agentskills.io/specification) | [What are skills?](https://agentskills.io/what-are-skills)\n- **BMad Method:** [bmad-code-org/BMAD-METHOD](https://github.com/bmad-code-org/BMAD-METHOD)\n\n---\n\n*Your skills get smarter every time you forge one. That's Deep tier.*\n"
}
```

     1→# Your Agent Skills Get Smarter Every Time You Forge One. Here's How.
     2→
     3→*Inside Skill Forge's Deep tier — where compilation builds a searchable knowledge graph, automatically.*
     4→
     5→---
     6→
     7→## The Intelligence Gap
     8→
     9→You've compiled an agent skill. It's good — AST-verified signatures, provenance citations, confidence tiers. Your agent stops hallucinating that library's API. Problem solved.
    10→
    11→But what happens when you compile your *second* skill? Your *tenth*? When you audit a skill six weeks later and need to understand how the source has drifted?
    12→
    13→Each skill compilation produces rich, structured data: 354 verified exports, co-import patterns, confidence ratings, provenance maps. Then that intelligence sits in files on disk — invisible to every subsequent workflow. The next audit-skill run starts from scratch. The next update-skill extraction has no memory of what came before.
    14→
    15→The data exists. It's curated, high-confidence, structurally verified. But nothing connects it.
    16→
    17→What if every skill you compiled made every future workflow smarter?
    18→
    19→---
    20→
    21→## Deep Tier: Where Compilation Builds Knowledge
    22→
    23→Skill Forge's progressive capability model has three tiers:
    24→
    25→- **Quick** — GitHub CLI reads source files. Best-effort extraction.
    26→- **Forge** — ast-grep parses the Abstract Syntax Tree. Structural truth, T1 confidence.
    27→- **Deep** — ast-grep + GitHub CLI + QMD. Everything Forge provides, plus a searchable knowledge layer that grows with every skill you create.
    28→
    29→Quick and Forge produce excellent skills. Deep does something fundamentally different: it turns your skill compilation pipeline into a **progressive knowledge graph**.
    30→
    31→Here's what that means in practice.
    32→
    33→---
    34→
    35→## Progressive Collection Building
    36→
    37→When you compile a skill in Deep tier, the workflow doesn't just write files to disk. After generating all seven artifacts — SKILL.md, context-snippet, metadata, references, provenance map, evidence report, extraction rules — it indexes the curated output into a QMD collection.
    38→
    39→```
    40→Artifacts generated.
    41→
    42→Deliverables (skills/payment-service/):
    43→- SKILL.md
    44→- context-snippet.md
    45→- metadata.json
    46→- references/ (12 files)
    47→
    48→Workspace (forge-data/payment-service/):
    49→- provenance-map.json
    50→- evidence-report.md
    51→- extraction-rules.yaml
    52→
    53→QMD collection registered: payment-service-extraction
    54→```
    55→
    56→That last line is the Deep tier difference. The structured, confidence-rated extraction data — every function signature, every type definition, every co-import pattern — is now searchable via QMD's hybrid search engine. Keyword matching. Semantic search. LLM-powered re-ranking.
    57→
    58→When you brief a skill, the same thing happens:
    59→
    60→```
    61→Skill brief written successfully.
    62→
    63→File: forge-data/payment-service/skill-brief.yaml
    64→QMD collection registered: payment-service-brief
    65→```
    66→
    67→Every workflow step that produces an artifact enriches the searchable knowledge base as a natural side-effect of doing its job. No extra commands. No manual indexing. No configuration.
    68→
    69→You compile skills. Deep tier builds knowledge.
    70→
    71→---
    72→
    73→## Why Curated Data Beats Raw Source
    74→
    75→The intuitive approach to search is "index everything." Clone the repo, dump all source files into a search engine, let users query freely. It sounds comprehensive. It's actually noisy.
    76→
    77→Consider a real extraction from a knowledge graph framework — 197 Python source files containing infrastructure adapters, internal utilities, test helpers, `__init__.py` boilerplate, and the actual public API surface. A raw source index treats all of these equally. Search for "add data" and you get import statements, private helper functions, variable assignments, and — buried somewhere in the results — the actual public function you wanted.
    78→
    79→Now consider what the extraction pipeline produces from those same 197 files:
    80→
    81→```
    82→Files scanned: ~197 unique Python files
    83→Exports found: ~354 unique entries
    84→  - Functions: ~120 (async functions, static methods)
    85→  - Classes: ~140 (models, adapters, engines, handlers)
    86→  - Types/Enums: ~30
    87→  - Constants/Singletons: ~10
    88→  - Data Models (Pydantic/SQLAlchemy): ~54
    89→
    90→Confidence: 354 T1 (source-reading verified), 0 T1-low
    91→```
    92→
    93→Each export carries its full signature, file path, line number, parameter types, return type, and confidence tier. The extraction pipeline has already done the hard work of separating signal from noise. When this curated data gets indexed into QMD, every search returns *the right thing* — because there's no wrong thing in the collection.
    94→
    95→Search "add data" against the extraction collection and you get:
    96→
    97→```
    98→async def add(data, dataset_name, ...) — ingest data
    99→[AST:cognee/__init__.py:L42] Confidence: T1
   100→```
   101→
   102→One result. The right result. With provenance.
   103→
   104→This is the core insight: **index the output of intelligence, not the input to it**.
   105→
   106→---
   107→
   108→## The Registry: Connective Tissue
   109→
   110→Collections don't float in the void. Every QMD collection created by a workflow is registered in `forge-tier.yaml` — the same file that tracks tool availability and capability tier:
   111→
   112→```yaml
   113→tools:
   114→  ast_grep: true
   115→  gh_cli: true
   116→  qmd: true
   117→
   118→tier: Deep
   119→tier_detected_at: "2026-03-14T10:30:00Z"
   120→
   121→qmd_collections:
   122→  - name: "payment-service-extraction"
   123→    type: "extraction"
   124→    source_workflow: "create-skill"
   125→    skill_name: "payment-service"
   126→    created_at: "2026-03-14"
   127→  - name: "payment-service-brief"
   128→    type: "brief"
   129→    source_workflow: "brief-skill"
   130→    skill_name: "payment-service"
   131→    created_at: "2026-03-14"
   132→```
   133→
   134→Six lines per collection. The registry serves as the single source of truth for what's been indexed. Downstream workflows don't guess — they read the registry, find collections by skill name and type, and query precisely what they need.
   135→
   136→This decoupling is deliberate. The workflow that *creates* a collection (create-skill) has no knowledge of the workflows that *consume* it (audit-skill, update-skill). They communicate through the registry. Add a new producer? Consumers discover it automatically. Add a new consumer? Existing producers don't change.

   147→

   150→- What is its usage frequency across the codebase?
   151→- How does the current extraction compare to the previously compiled skill content?
   152→
   153→The drift report goes from "something changed" to "here's what changed, when it changed, and how significant the change is." T1 structural truth enriched with T2 temporal context.

   250→## The Deep Tier Lifecycle

   253→

   255→

Deep tier indexes the curated output into a `{name}-extraction` collection and registers it. The extraction data — structured markdown with one section per export — is now searchable.
   265→
   266→**Step 4: Audit** — `@Ferris AS`
   267→
   268→Six weeks later, the source has evolved. Ferris re-extracts the current state, then queries the extraction collection for temporal context. The drift report shows not just *what* changed, but *when* and *how significantly*. T1 structural truth enriched with T2 temporal intelligence.
   269→
   270→**Step 5: Update** — `@Ferris US`
   271→
   272→Ferris re-extracts only changed files, queries the extraction collection for semantic context, and merges fresh data with the existing skill — preserving your `[MANUAL]` sections. The updated extraction collection replaces the old one. The registry entry is updated. The knowledge graph stays current.
   273→
   274→Each step builds on the previous. Each step leaves the system smarter for the next. This is progressive collection building — intelligence that compounds.
   275→
   276→---
   277→
   278→## What This Means for You
   279→
   280→If you're using Quick or Forge tier, nothing changes. Your skills are complete, accurate, and useful at their tier. The progressive capability model means every tier produces legitimate output.
   281→
   282→If you install QMD and activate Deep tier, you unlock a layer that Quick and Forge can't provide:
   283→
   284→- **Audit context** — Drift reports enriched with temporal intelligence
   285→- **Update precision** — Surgical regeneration informed by usage patterns
   286→- **Portfolio search** — Cross-skill deduplication across your entire project
   287→- **Self-maintaining collections** — Hygiene runs automatically, orphans are flagged, stale entries cleaned
   288→- **Compound intelligence** — Every skill you compile makes every future workflow smarter
   289→
   290→The setup is three tools:
   291→
   292→```bash
   293→# ast-grep for structural extraction
   294→brew install ast-grep  # or cargo install ast-grep
   295→
   296→# GitHub CLI for source access
   297→brew install gh
   298→
   299→# QMD for hybrid search
   300→# See https://github.com/tobi/qmd
   301→```
   302→
   303→Then:
   304→
   305→```
   306→@Ferris SF    # Detects all three tools, sets tier to Deep
   307→@Ferris BS    # Brief — indexed automatically
   308→@Ferris CS    # Compile — indexed automatically
   309→```
   310→
   311→Your knowledge graph starts building itself.
   312→
   313→---
   314→
   315→## Try It
   316→
   317→```bash
   318→npx bmad-module-skill-forge install
   319→```
   320→
   321→Setup your forge, brief a skill, compile it. If QMD is available, Deep tier activates automatically. Every compilation enriches the searchable knowledge base. Every audit gets temporal context. Every update gets semantic intelligence.
   322→
   323→No configuration. No manual indexing. No maintenance.
   324→
   325→Just compile skills. Deep tier handles the rest.
   326→
